Spectral Intertwining Operators
Qiyuan Chen

Abstract
We study spectral intertwining operators between spectral Eisenstein series Eis P ∨ \operatorname{Eis}_{P^{\vee}} , Eis Q ∨ \operatorname{Eis}_{Q^{\vee}} for two parabolic subgroups P , Q P,Q of a p p -adic reductive group G G with the same Levi subgroup M M , inspired by the analogy with the classical intertwining operators between parabolic induced representations of p p -adic reductive groups. In particular, we construct the normalized (canonical) intertwining operator that satisfies the transitivity and an unnormalized intertwining operator that is adjoint to a rational section of an analog of the Bruhat–Mackey’s filtration. Moreover, the normalized and unnormalized intertwining operators differ by a ratio of L-functions, analogously to the Langlands conjecture about classical ones up to units. Finally we prove that the spectral intertwining operators correspond to classical ones up to units under any conjectural categorical local Langlands correspondence.



1
Introduction

1.1
Motivation

This article is concerned with the intertwining operators on the spectral side of the Langlands correspondence. Firstly, we briefly recall the classical intertwining operators. Let
F
be a
p
-adic local field and
G
be a reductive group over
F
. Suppose
G
has a Levi subgroup
M
and two parabolic subgroups
(P,Q)
with Levi component
M
. There is a family of (rational) homomorphisms between parabolic inductions
i_{P}^{G}
and
i_{Q}^{G}
, called unnormalized intertwining operators:

J_{Q|P}(\pi)\colon i_{P}^{G}(\pi)\to i_{Q}^{G}(\pi)

where
\pi
is a irreducible representation of
M(F)
over some appropriate field, such that the composition

\pi\to r_{Q}^{G}\circ i_{P}^{G}(\pi)/F^{>1}\to\pi

of the inclusion (given by the Mackey’s formula) and the adjunction of
J_{Q|P}
is the identity, where
F^{>1}
is a submodule of
r_{Q}^{G}\circ i_{P}^{G}(\pi)
defined by the Bruhat–Mackey’s filtration. The intertwining operator is a classical object in the representation theory of
p
-adic reductive groups, which will reflect the semisimplicity of representations such as
i_{P}^{G}\pi
and
r_{Q}^{G}\circ i_{P}^{G}\pi
.

Though the definition of the intertwining operators is natural, they usually do not satisfy the transitivity
J_{R|Q}\circ J_{Q|P}=J_{R|P}
for another parabolic
R
with Levi component
M
,
which is useful to decompose parabolically induced representations.
The Langlands conjecture (
[
13
]
, Appendix II) measures this failure: it is necessary to compatibly multiplying the unnormalized intertwining operators by some rational functions to obtain the transitivity. More precisely:

Conjecture 1.1.1

(Langlands)
.

For irreducible representations
\pi
,
the normalized intertwining operators

J_{Q|P}^{\circ}(\pi):=\gamma(0,\pi,\mathrm{Ad}_{{\mathfrak{n}}_{Q^{\vee}}/{\mathfrak{n}}_{P^{\vee}}})J_{Q|P}(\pi):=\epsilon(0,\pi,\mathrm{Ad}_{{\mathfrak{n}}_{Q^{\vee}}/{\mathfrak{n}}_{P^{\vee}}})\frac{L(1,\pi,\mathrm{Ad}_{{\mathfrak{n}}_{Q^{\vee}}/{\mathfrak{n}}_{P^{\vee}}})}{L(0,\pi,\mathrm{Ad}_{{\mathfrak{n}}_{Q^{\vee}}/{\mathfrak{n}}_{P^{\vee}}})}J_{Q|P}(\pi),

where the
\epsilon
and
L
’s denote the conjectural
\epsilon
-factor and L-functions for automorphic representations,
satisfy the transitivity

J^{\circ}_{R|Q}\circ J^{\circ}_{Q|P}=J^{\circ}_{R|P}.

Shahidi proposed a construction of the
\epsilon
-factors and
L
-functions for automorphic representations (
[
14
]
, Theorem 7.9) that satisfy the Langlands conjecture purely on the automorphic side. A natural question is to ask whether under a proposed construction of local Langlands correspondence
\pi\mapsto\phi_{\pi}
, such as the construction of Fargues–Scholze (
[
5
]
), the L-functions defined by
L(s,\pi,V):=L(s,\phi_{\pi},V)
via the Galois representations and similarly the
\epsilon
-factors will satisfy the Langlands conjecture. Note that the L-parameter
\phi_{\pi}
by Fargues–Scholze is the “semisimplified” parameter and thus the
L(s,\phi_{\pi},V)
is not the classical
L(s,\pi,V)
in general.

The main goal of this article is to construct similar intertwining operators on the spectral side of the local Langlands correspondence,
and then verify the Langlands conjecture under the conjectural categorical local Langlands correspondence.
For this purpose, we firstly introduce the corresponding objects on the spectral side that are concerned in the theory of intertwining operators.

Denote by
G^{*}
the quasisplit inner form of
G
. We will always assume that
G
is an extended pure inner form:
G=(G^{*})_{b}
for an element
b
in the Kottwitz set
B(G^{*})_{bas}
.
Recall that the categorical Langlands correspondence, conjectured by Fargues–Scholze (
[
5
]
, Conjecture X.1.4),
(which is compatible with the set-theoretical correspondence
\pi\mapsto\phi_{\pi}
in loc. cit.)
says that over
\overline{\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}}
, there should be an equivalence of
\infty
-categories:

D_{\text{lis}}(\operatorname{Bun}_{G^{*}})^{\omega}\simeq D\operatorname{Coh}^{\text{qc}}(\operatorname{Par}_{G^{\vee}}),

where:

•

D_{\text{lis}}(\operatorname{Bun}_{G^{*}})^{\omega}
is the subcategory of the compact objects in the category of lisse étale sheaves over the moduli space of principal
G^{*}
-bundles over the Fargues–Fontaine curve.

•

D\operatorname{Coh}^{\text{qc}}(\operatorname{Par}_{G^{\vee}})

is the category of derived coherent sheaves with quasi-compact support over the moduli space of
L
-parameters of the Langlands dual of
G^{*}
,
{G^{*}}^{\vee}\simeq G^{\vee}
.

The category
D(\mathsf{Rep}(G(F))
of representations of
G(F)
can be viewed as a full subcategory of
D(\operatorname{Bun}_{G^{*},b})
, for which Hamann, Hansen and Scholze (
[
6
]
) constructed the “geometric Eisenstein operators”
\operatorname{Eis}_{P!}
that extend the parabolic induction functors
i_{P}^{G}
.
Moreover, it is conjectured (
[
8
]
, Conjecture 5.4.1) that
under the conjectural categorical local Langlands correspondence,
the geometric Eisenstein operator
\operatorname{Eis}_{\bar{P}!}
(where bar denotes the opposite parabolic subgroup) corresponds to the following spectral Eisenstein series functor

\operatorname{Eis}_{P^{\vee}}=p_{P*}q^{*}_{P}\colon D\operatorname{Coh}(\operatorname{Par}_{M^{\vee}})\to D\operatorname{Coh}(\operatorname{Par}_{G^{\vee}})

where
p_{P}\colon\operatorname{Par}_{P^{\vee}}\to\operatorname{Par}_{G^{\vee}}
and
q_{P}\colon\operatorname{Par}_{P^{\vee}}\to\operatorname{Par}_{M^{\vee}}
are induced by the natural inclusion
P^{\vee}\to G^{\vee}
and projection
P^{\vee}\to M^{\vee}
respectively. Thus it is natural to hope that there are natural “rational” transformations

J_{Q^{\vee}|P^{\vee}}^{\circ},J_{Q^{\vee}|P^{\vee}}\colon\operatorname{Eis}_{P^{\vee}}\to\operatorname{Eis}_{Q^{\vee}}

serving as the normalized and unnormalized “spectral intertwining operator” respectively and satisfying an analog of the Langlands conjecture.

1.2
Main results

Now we state the main theorems of the article. Our first surprising result is that unlike the classical case, the normalized intertwining operator is the one that appears the most naturally on the spectral side, as a canonical isomorphism between Eisenstein operators on an open substack of the moduli of parameters.

Theorem 1.2.1

(Intertwining over the regular semisimple and generic locus)
.

After restricting to the regular semisimple and generic loci

\operatorname{Par}_{M^{\vee}}^{rs,g}
,

\operatorname{Par}_{P^{\vee}}^{rs,g}
,
\operatorname{Par}_{Q^{\vee}}^{rs,g}
and
\operatorname{Par}_{G^{\vee}}^{rs,g}

(which come from dense open subsets in corresponding coarse moduli spaces),
we obtain operators

\operatorname{Eis}_{P^{\vee}}^{rs,g},\operatorname{Eis}_{Q^{\vee}}^{rs,g}\colon D\operatorname{Coh}(\operatorname{Par}_{M^{\vee}}^{rs,g})\to D\operatorname{Coh}(\operatorname{Par}_{G^{\vee}}^{rs,g}).

Then there is a canonical isomorphism, called the normalized intertwining operator,

J^{\circ}_{Q^{\vee}|P^{\vee}}=J^{rs,g}_{Q^{\vee}|P^{\vee}}\colon\operatorname{Eis}^{rs,g}_{P^{\vee}}\operatorname{\xrightarrow{\sim}}\operatorname{Eis}^{rs,g}_{Q^{\vee}}

satisfying the transitivity for another parabolic
R

J^{\circ}_{R^{\vee}|Q^{\vee}}\circ J^{\circ}_{Q^{\vee}|P^{\vee}}=J^{\circ}_{R^{\vee}|P^{\vee}}.

Moreover,
J^{\circ}_{Q^{\vee}|P^{\vee}}
generates the free
{\mathcal{Z}}(D\operatorname{Coh}(\operatorname{Par}_{M^{\vee}}^{rs,g}))
-module

\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{Eis}^{rs,g}_{P^{\vee}},\operatorname{Eis}^{rs,g}_{Q^{\vee}})
.

The idea for proving this theorem is that
\operatorname{Eis}_{P^{\vee}}^{rs,g}
is canonically isomorphic to
\iota_{M^{\vee},G^{\vee}*}
, where

\iota_{M^{\vee},G^{\vee}}\colon\operatorname{Par}_{M^{\vee}}^{rs,g}\to\operatorname{Par}_{G^{\vee}}^{rs,g}

is induced by the inclusion
M^{\vee}\to G^{\vee}
.

In this theorem, the regular semisimple locus and the generic locus are defined in Definition
2.2.3
and
2.2.1
in the unipotent case and Definition
3.2.2
in general. We remark that similar loci are defined in
[
7
]
,

[
9
]
and
[
20
]
.
The isomorphism
J^{\circ}
is defined in Definition
2.2.11
in the unipotent case and Definition
3.2.8
in general. The property about generating is proved in Proposition
2.2.13
in the unipotent case and Proposition
3.2.9
in general.

Our second result is that after multiplying by the inverse of some L-function at
1
, seen as a regular function on the moduli space of parameters
\operatorname{Par}_{M^{\vee}}
(see Definition
3.2.1
) and restricting to some blocks, the normalized intertwining operator can be uniquely extended to the whole space and thus provides an ‘integral intertwining operator”. Here, the inverse of the L-function at
1
appears naturally, as the function of definition (up to a unit) of some components of the “non-generic” locus of
\operatorname{Par}_{M^{\vee}}
.

More precisely, let
\phi
be an elliptic semisimple parameter in
\operatorname{Par}_{M^{\vee}}
for a Levi subgroup
M^{\vee}\subset G^{\vee}
.
Consider the Eisenstein operator on the connected component containing
\phi
:

\operatorname{Eis}_{P^{\vee}}\colon D\operatorname{Coh}(\operatorname{Par}_{M^{\vee},\phi})\to D\operatorname{Coh}(\operatorname{Par}_{G^{\vee},\phi})

Since
\operatorname{Par}_{M^{\vee},\phi}
is a
C_{M^{\vee}}(\phi)
-gerbe over its coarse moduli space (see Lemma
3.3.1
), we have a block decomposition of
D\operatorname{Coh}(\operatorname{Par}_{M^{\vee},\phi})
by isotypic components. Let
V
be an irreducible representation of
C_{M^{\vee}}(\phi)
that it is trivial on
C_{M^{\vee}}(\phi)^{\circ}
.

Theorem 1.2.2

(Integral intertwining)
.

After restricting to the
V
-isotypic block

D\operatorname{Coh}(\operatorname{Par}_{M^{\vee},\phi})_{V}
,

•

The
{\mathcal{O}}(\operatorname{Par}_{M^{\vee},\phi})
-module

\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{Eis}_{P^{\vee}},\operatorname{Eis}_{Q^{\vee}})
of all transformations between Eisenstein operators is free and of rank
1
.

•

There is a unique generator of this module, called the integral intertwining operator

j_{Q^{\vee}|P^{\vee}}\colon\operatorname{Eis}_{P^{\vee}}\to\operatorname{Eis}_{Q^{\vee}}

which restricts to
L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{{\mathfrak{n}}_{Q^{\vee}}/{\mathfrak{n}}_{P^{\vee}}})^{-1}
times
J^{\circ}_{Q^{\vee}|P^{\vee}}
on the regular semisimple and generic locus.

This theorem is proved in Proposition
2.4.1
in the unipotent case and Theorem
3.4.1
in general.

Our third theorem states that after dividing by the L-function at
0
, the integral intertwining operator will satisfy similar properties as the classical unnormalized intertwining operators, i.e., its adjoint induces a rational section of an analog of the Mackey’s filtration.

Theorem 1.2.3

(Adjunction)
.

After dividing by the L-function
L(0,\mathrm{Ad}_{{\mathfrak{n}}_{Q^{\vee}}/{\mathfrak{n}}_{P^{\vee}}})^{-1}
, the transformation
j_{Q^{\vee}|P^{\vee}}
becomes a rational operator
J_{Q^{\vee}|P^{\vee}}
,
called the unnormalized spectral intertwining operator,
satisfying that the composition

\mathbb{1}\to\operatorname{CT}_{Q^{\vee}}\circ\operatorname{Eis}_{P^{\vee}}/F^{>1}\to\mathbb{1}

is an isomorphism, where

•

\operatorname{CT}
is the (partially defined) left adjoint of
\operatorname{Eis}
;

•

F^{>1}
is defined by some filtration of
\operatorname{CT}\circ\operatorname{Eis}
;

•

The
{\mathcal{O}}(\operatorname{Par}_{M,\phi})
-module
\operatorname{Hom}(\mathbb{1},\operatorname{CT}_{Q^{\vee}}\circ\operatorname{Eis}_{P^{\vee}}/F^{>1})
is free and of rank
1

and the first transformation is a generator of it;

•

The second transformation is induced by the adjunction of
J_{Q^{\vee}|P^{\vee}}
.

Remark 1.2.4
.

Recently, Hansen–Mann (
[
8
]
, Definition 3.5.1 and Theorem 3.6.3) defined a functor
\operatorname{CT}
and a filtration on
\operatorname{CT}\circ\operatorname{Eis}
. However, their construction is not much related to ours, though the notations are similar. Their
\operatorname{CT}
is the right adjoint of
\operatorname{Eis}
but ours is the (partially defined) left adjoint. Moreover, the subquotient indexed by
w\in W^{\sigma}
of the filtration in our case is
w^{*}
, which is simpler than the subquotient in their case, as we are restricted to some special blocks.

The inverse of the L-function at
0
appears naturally here, as the function of definition (up to a unit) of some components of the “non-regular semisimple” locus of
\operatorname{Par}_{M^{\vee},\phi}
.
In this theorem, the adjoint functor
\operatorname{CT}
is defined in Proposition
2.5.7
,
2.5.8
in the unipotent case and
3.5.2
in general; the filtration is defined in
2.5.10
in the unipotent case and
3.5.6
in general.
This theorem is proved in Theorem
2.5.13
in the unipotent case and
3.5.7
in general.

By construction,
the following analog of the Langlands conjecture holds:
the operator

\frac{L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{{\mathfrak{n}}_{Q^{\vee}}/{\mathfrak{n}}_{P^{\vee}}})}{L(0,\mathrm{Ad}_{{\mathfrak{n}}_{Q^{\vee}}/{\mathfrak{n}}_{P^{\vee}}})}J_{Q^{\vee}|P^{\vee}}

satisfies the transitivity (as it is identified with
J^{\circ}_{Q^{\vee}|P^{\vee}}
).
In particular, the composition
J_{P^{\vee}|Q^{\vee}}\circ J_{Q^{\vee}|P^{\vee}}
is given by the multiplication of the function

\phi\mapsto\frac{L(0,\phi,\mathrm{Ad}_{{\mathfrak{n}}_{P^{\vee}}/{\mathfrak{n}}_{Q^{\vee}}})L(0,\phi,\mathrm{Ad}_{{\mathfrak{n}}_{Q^{\vee}}/{\mathfrak{n}}_{P^{\vee}}})}{L(1,\phi,\mathrm{Ad}_{{\mathfrak{n}}_{P^{\vee}}/{\mathfrak{n}}_{Q^{\vee}}})L(1,\phi,\mathrm{Ad}_{{\mathfrak{n}}_{Q^{\vee}}/{\mathfrak{n}}_{P^{\vee}}})}

on
\operatorname{Par}_{M^{\vee},\phi}
. We remark that these theorems are purely on the spectral side.
Finally, we prove that (Theorem
4.0.5
) under the categorical Langlands correspondence, the spectral intertwining operator
J_{Q^{\vee}|P^{\vee}}
corresponds to the classical intertwining operator
J_{\bar{Q}|\bar{P}}
of representations
up to a unit
in
{\mathcal{O}}(\operatorname{Par}_{M^{\vee},\phi})
, verifying the Langlands conjecture
1.1.1
up to a unit for supercuspidal representations with elliptic L-parameter.

Remark 1.2.5
.

Though there is an opposition of parabolic subgroup
\operatorname{Eis}_{P!}\longleftrightarrow\operatorname{Eis}_{\bar{P}^{\vee}}
under the conjectural categorical local Langlands correspondence, our result is still compatible with the Langlands conjecture. In the convention in
[
8
]
, the categorical local Langlands correspondence is compatible with the local class field theory
F^{\times}\simeq W_{F}^{ab}
which sends the uniformizer
\varpi
of
F^{\times}
to the
geometric
Frobenius.
Hence we should use the geometric Frobenius in the expected formula for the automorphic L-function

L(s,\pi,V)=L(s,\phi_{\pi},V)=\det(1-q^{-s}\phi_{\pi}(\mathrm{Fr}),V^{I})^{-1}.

However, in our conventions in section 2 and section 3, the Frobenius in
W_{F}
is taken to be the
arithmetic
Frobenius, which is the inverse of the geometric Frobenius.
By definition,

L(s,\phi,\mathrm{Ad}_{{\mathfrak{n}}_{P^{\vee}}})=\det(1-q^{-s}\phi(\mathrm{Fr}),\mathrm{Ad}_{{\mathfrak{n}}_{P^{\vee}}^{I}})^{-1}=\det(1-q^{-s}\phi(\mathrm{Fr}^{-1}),\mathrm{Ad}_{{\mathfrak{n}}_{\bar{P}^{\vee}}^{I}})^{-1}.

Thus the expected automorphic L-function
L(s,\pi,\mathrm{Ad}_{{\mathfrak{n}}_{\bar{P}^{\vee}}})
is
L(s,\phi_{\pi},\mathrm{Ad}_{{\mathfrak{n}}_{P^{\vee}}})
in our convention, explaining the compatibility.

Remark 1.2.6
.

Note that our construction of spectral intertwining operators
j_{Q|P}
and
J_{Q|P}
depends on the arbitrary choices of a generator of the corresponding
\operatorname{Hom}
-modules, which are unique up to a unit in
{\mathcal{O}}(\operatorname{Par}_{M^{\vee},\phi})
. Thus our result about the composition of intertwining operators differs from the Langlands conjecture by some epsilon factors, which are units.

We do not have a good explanation of the unit here. It may rely on finding a corresponding object on the spectral side of the integration which appears in the definition of the inclusion
\mathbb{1}\to r_{Q}^{G}\circ i_{P}^{G}/F^{>1}
in the Mackey’s formula.

This article is divided into three sections. The first section deals with the unipotent component of unramified reductive groups, which can be treated by the “twisted Springer theory” developed by Xiao–Zhu (
[
17
]
). The second section deals with the general case, which can be reduced to the case of principal blocks using the structure results about
\operatorname{Par}_{G}
stated in
[
3
]
. In the last section we prove the compatibility of the spectral intertwining operators and the classical intertwining operators under the conjectural categorical local Langlands correspondence.

The author expresses his deepest gratitude to his Ph.D. supervisor Jean-François Dat for introducing the subject of intertwining operators and local Langlands correspondence and very helpful discussions and corrections about this article. 2
The unipotent component

In this section, let
G
be a reductive group over some algebraically closed field
k
of characteristic
0
,
B
be a Borel subgroup of
G
and
T
be a torus of
B
. Let
\sigma
be an automorphism of
G
fixing
B
,
T
and a pinning of
G
. We note that these data may come from the Langlands dual of some quasisplit reductive group over a
p
-adic local field
F
.

From the pair
(G,\sigma)
, we construct the semidirect product
G\rtimes\langle\sigma\rangle
of
G
and the group generated by
\sigma
. We will consider
G\sigma:=\{(g\in G,\sigma)\}
, a subscheme of the product. Then for
h\in G
, viewed as
(h,1)\in G\rtimes\langle\sigma\rangle
, the adjoint action of
h
on
G\sigma
is given by
h(g\sigma)h^{-1}=hg\sigma(h)^{-1}\sigma
.

We define the scheme

\tilde{{\mathbb{L}}}_{G\sigma}:=\{(g\sigma,n)\in G\sigma\times{\mathcal{N}}\mid\mathrm{Ad}(g)(\sigma(n))=qn\}

and its quotient stack

{\mathbb{L}}_{G\sigma}:=\{(g\sigma,n)\in G\sigma\times{\mathcal{N}}\mid\mathrm{Ad}(g)(\sigma(n))=qn\}/G

where
q=p^{n}\in k
for some prime
p
and
n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{+}
and
{\mathcal{N}}
is the nilpotent cone of
G
. The
G
-action here is given by
h(g,n):=(hg\sigma(h)^{-1},\mathrm{Ad}(h)(n))
.
Similarly we define
{\mathbb{L}}_{B\sigma}
and
{\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma}
and note that
{\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma}\simeq T\sigma/T
. Note that
{\mathbb{L}}_{G\sigma}
is isomorphic to the so-called “unipotent” component
\operatorname{Par}_{G,1}
containing the unipotent representations in the moduli space of Weil–Deligne L-parameters
\operatorname{Par}_{G}^{\text{WD}}
(which is isomorphic to
\operatorname{Par}_{G}:=Z^{1}(W_{F},G)/G
by
[
5
]
, Proposition VIII.2.5) containing those L-parameters whose semisimplifications are unramified if we take
q
be the cardinal of the residue field of the local field
F
.
Here the
W_{F}
-action on
G
satisfies that the inertia subgroup acts trivially on
G
and the action of the
arithmetic
Frobenius is given by
\sigma
. The identification
\operatorname{Par}_{G,1}\operatorname{\xrightarrow{\sim}}{\mathbb{L}}_{G\sigma}
is given by

(\phi,N)\mapsto(\phi(\mathrm{Fr})\sigma,N),

where
\mathrm{Fr}
is the arithmetic Frobenius.
We remark that the object
{\mathbb{L}}_{G\sigma}
is considered in
[
10
]
(where
q
is replaced by
q^{-1}
) and
[
1
]
when
\sigma=1
.

Next we define the Eisenstein series.

Definition 2.0.1

(Spectral Eisenstein series)
.

For any Borel subgroup
B\subset G
containing
T
and fixed by
\sigma
, define

p_{B}\colon{\mathbb{L}}_{B\sigma}\to{\mathbb{L}}_{G\sigma}
induced by the inclusion
B\to G
and

q_{B}\colon{\mathbb{L}}_{B\sigma}\to{\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma}
induced by the projection
B\to T
. The spectral Eisenstein operator
\operatorname{Eis}_{B}
is defined as

\operatorname{Eis}_{B}:=p_{B*}\circ q_{B}^{*}\colon D\operatorname{Coh}({\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma})\to D\operatorname{Coh}({\mathbb{L}}_{G\sigma}).

Note that the morphism
p_{B}
is proper by
[
18
]
, Lemma 3.21. Thus
\operatorname{Eis}_{B}
will send
D\operatorname{Coh}
to
D\operatorname{Coh}
.

We will also need a variant of the spectral Eisenstein series by ignoring the monodromy part.

Definition 2.0.2

(Variant)
.

For any Borel subgroup
B\subset G
containing
T
and fixed by
\sigma
, abusing the notations, define

p_{B,0}\colon B\sigma/B\to G\sigma/G
(denoted by
p_{0}
is there is no ambiguity) induced by the inclusion
B\to G
and

q_{B,0}\colon B\sigma/B\to T\sigma/T
(denoted by
q_{0}
is there is no ambiguity) induced by the projection
B\to T
. The variant spectral Eisenstein operator
\operatorname{eis}_{B}
is defined as

\operatorname{eis}_{B}:=p_{0*}\circ q_{0}^{*}\colon D\operatorname{Coh}(T\sigma/T)\to D\operatorname{Coh}(G\sigma/G).

Let
B^{\prime}
be another Borel subgroup of
G
fixed by
\sigma
and containing
T
.
We will construct the spectral intertwining operator

J_{B^{\prime}|B}\colon\operatorname{Eis}_{B}\to\operatorname{Eis}_{B^{\prime}}

in the following steps:

•

Define two open subsets of
{\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma}
with respect to
G
, called the regular semisimple locus and the generic locus. Then construct the intertwining operator after restricting to the intersection of these loci. The operator is the normalized intertwining operator
J^{\circ}_{Q|P}
.

•

Extend the operator to the generic locus.

•

After multiplication by some function on
{\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma}
, extend the operator to all
\operatorname{Par}_{T}
and one obtains an operator
j_{Q|P}
defined on all
\operatorname{Par}_{T}
.

•

Calculate the adjunction
\operatorname{CT}\circ\operatorname{Eis}\to\mathbb{1}
, construct an operator

\mathbb{1}\to\operatorname{CT}\circ\operatorname{Eis}/F
for some subfunctor
F
and calculate their composition to get a function in
{\mathcal{O}}({\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma})
. Divide by this function and one obtains the unnormalized intertwining operator
J_{Q|P}
.

2.1
Some results about the pair
(G,\sigma)

We recall some basic results about reductive groups with an automorphism preserving a fixed pinning of it. The proofs of those results can be found in the article of Xiao–Zhu (
[
17
]
).

Proposition 2.1.1

(The root system for
\sigma
,
[
17
]
Lemma 5.1.1)
.

\sigma
permutes the roots of
G
. Moreover, there exists a root system for the torus
T_{\sigma}
and weight lattice
X^{*}(T_{\sigma})\simeq X^{*}(T)^{\sigma}
where the roots are of the form

\alpha_{O}:=\sum_{\beta\in O}\beta\in X^{*}(T)^{\sigma}

where
O
is an orbit of the
\sigma
-action on roots of
G
.
Moreover, the Weyl group of this root system is
W^{\sigma}
. The Weyl group of the root system can also be identified as

N_{G}(T\sigma)/T=\{g\in G\mid g(T\sigma)g^{-1}\subset T\sigma\}/T=\{g\in N_{G}(T)\mid\sigma(g)g^{-1}\in T\}/T.

From the construction, the roots in the root system can be viewed as weights of the torus
T_{\sigma}
.

Definition 2.1.2

(Type of an orbit,
[
17
]
Definition 5.1.4)
.

•

If
\alpha_{O}
is not equal to
\alpha_{O^{\prime}}
for other orbits
O^{\prime}
,

O
is called of type A.

•

If there exists an another
O^{\prime}
such that
\alpha_{O}=\alpha_{O^{\prime}}
,

O^{\prime}
is unique. Moreover,
|O|=2|O^{\prime}|
or
|O^{\prime}|=2|O|
. We call the orbit consisting less elements of type BC+ and the other of type BC- and we say
\alpha_{O}
is of type BC.

Denote
{\mathfrak{n}}
the Lie algebra of the unipotent radical of
B
. Then there is an adjoint action of
T
on
{\mathfrak{n}}
, denoted
\mathrm{Ad}_{\mathfrak{n}}\colon T\to\operatorname{End}({\mathfrak{n}})
.
We consider the function in
{\mathcal{O}}(T)

L(s,\mathrm{Ad}_{\mathfrak{n}}):=t\mapsto\det(1-q^{-s}\mathrm{Ad}_{\mathfrak{n}}(t)\circ\sigma)^{-1}

for
s=0,1
. The function is invariant under the twisted
T
-action, thus induces a function in
{\mathcal{O}}({\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma})
. We use the notation L because the function is the same as the L-function of Galois representations on the principal block. The notations in the L-function can be generalized as follows:
T
can be replaced by any subgroup of
G
and
{\mathfrak{n}}
can be replaced by any Lie subalgebra of
\mathfrak{g}
fixed by
\sigma
and the subgroup.
The next proposition expresses the
L
-function

L(s,\mathrm{Ad}_{\mathfrak{n}})
in terms of functions on the torus
T_{\sigma}
.

Proposition 2.1.3

(
L
-function)
.

The
L
-function can be expressed as

L(s,\mathrm{Ad}_{\mathfrak{n}})^{-1}=\prod_{O}L(s,\mathrm{Ad}_{{\mathfrak{n}}_{O}})^{-1}

where
O
is taken among the orbits of roots under the
\sigma
-action
and
{\mathfrak{n}}_{O}
is the direct sum of
{\mathfrak{n}}_{\alpha}
’s for
\alpha\in O
.
Moreover,

L(s,\mathrm{Ad}_{{\mathfrak{n}}}|_{O})^{-1}:=L(s,\mathrm{Ad}_{{\mathfrak{n}}_{O}})^{-1}=\begin{cases}1-q^{-s|O|}e^{\alpha_{O}}(t)&\text{$O$ is of type A or BC-}\\
1+q^{-s|O|}e^{\alpha_{O}}(t)&\text{$O$ is of type BC+}\end{cases}

(where
e^{\alpha}
is exactly
\alpha
, emphasizing the fact that it is a weight of
T_{\sigma}
).

Proof.

The case
s=0
is Lemma 5.2.9 in
[
17
]
and the proof works the same for
s=1
.
∎

The zeros of the
L
-function corresponds to the singular locus of the
W^{\sigma}
-action on
T_{\sigma}
.

Proposition 2.1.4

(
[
17
]
, Lemma 5.1.12)
.

Suppose that
G
is simply connected.
For a root
\alpha
of
T_{\sigma}
, the fixed point subgroup of
T_{\sigma}
under the reflection
s_{\alpha}
is the zero locus of the function

\begin{cases}1-e^{\alpha}(t)&\text{$\alpha$ is of type A}\\
(1+e^{\alpha}(t))(1-e^{\alpha}(t))&\text{$\alpha$ is of type BC}\end{cases}

and the multiplicity is
1
.

We have the following analog of the Chevalley isomorphism
G\sslash G\simeq T\sslash W
.

Proposition 2.1.5

(Twisted Chevalley isomorphism, Proposition 4.2.3 in
[
17
]
)
.

The natural inclusion
T\sigma\subset G\sigma
induces
an isomorphism
\chi\colon G\sigma\sslash G\simeq T\sigma\sslash N_{G}(T\sigma)
, called the twisted Chevalley isomorphism.

Moreover, we will call
G\sigma\to T\sigma\sslash N_{G}(T\sigma)
the twisted Chevalley map, denoted also by
\chi
.

2.2
The regular semisimple and generic locus

We firstly define the regular semisimple locus and the generic locus with respect to the
\sigma
-action.

Definition 2.2.1

(Generic locus)
.

We define the generic locus of
{\mathbb{L}}_{G\sigma}
,
{\mathbb{L}}_{B\sigma}
and
{\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma}
as follows.

•

For
T\sigma
, the generic locus of
T\sigma
,
(T\sigma)^{g}
is the non-vanishing locus of
L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{\mathfrak{n}})^{-1}\cdot L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{\bar{{\mathfrak{n}}}})^{-1}
, where
\bar{n}
is the opposite nilpotent subalgebra of
{\mathfrak{n}}
. Then we define the generic locus
{\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma}^{g}
of
{\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma}
as
(T\sigma)^{g}/T
. Note that
(T\sigma)^{g}
is stable under the action of the Weyl group
W^{\sigma}
and
T
-conjugation.

•

For
B\sigma
, we pull back the function
L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{\mathfrak{n}})^{-1}\cdot L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{\bar{{\mathfrak{n}}}})^{-1}
from
T
to
B
. The generic locus
(B\sigma)^{g}
of
B\sigma
is the non-vanishing locus of
L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{\mathfrak{n}})^{-1}\cdot L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{\bar{{\mathfrak{n}}}})^{-1}
.
From its construction,
(B\sigma)^{g}
is stable under
B
-conjugation.
Then the generic locus
{\mathbb{L}}_{B\sigma}^{g}
of
{\mathbb{L}}_{B\sigma}
is the (not necessarily dense) open substack containing those points
(b\sigma,n)
with
b\sigma\in(B\sigma)^{g}
.

•

For
G\sigma
, consider the twisted chevalley map

\chi\colon G\sigma\to(T\sigma)\sslash N_{G}(T\sigma)
. Then

(G\sigma)^{g}
is defined as
\chi^{-1}((T\sigma)^{g})
. From its construction,
(G\sigma)^{g}
is stable under
G
-conjugation.
Then the generic locus
{\mathbb{L}}_{G\sigma}^{g}
of
{\mathbb{L}}_{G\sigma}
is the (not necessarily dense) open substack containing those points
(g\sigma,n)
with
g\sigma\in(G\sigma)^{g}
.

We remark that the generic locus is not the open substack of generic parameters in the sense of Hamann (
[
7
]
defined by vanishing of Galois cohomology. The generic parameters in the sense of Hamann form the regular semisimple and generic locus in our sense, as seen in the proof of
2.2.6
.

We note that over the generic locus, the nilpotent part vanishes.

Lemma 2.2.2
.

The projection
{\mathbb{L}}_{G\sigma}^{g}\to(G\sigma)^{g}/G
is an isomorphism, similarly for
B
and
T
.

Proof.

For the argument about
G
, by the proof of
[
17
]
, Proposition 5.3.1,
B\sigma/B\to G\sigma/G
is surjective. Unwinding the definition, it suffices to prove that: for
b\in(B\sigma)^{g}
,
n\in{\mathcal{N}}
satisfying

\mathrm{Ad}(b)(n)=qn
, then
n=0
.

We have

L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{\mathfrak{g}}(b))^{-1}=\det(1-q^{-1}\mathrm{Ad}_{\mathfrak{g}}(b)\circ\sigma)=\det(1-q^{-1}\mathrm{Ad}_{\mathfrak{g}}(\bar{b})\circ\sigma)

where
\bar{b}
is the image of
B
in
T
via the projection
B\to T
. Thus

L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{\mathfrak{g}}(b))^{-1}=L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{\mathfrak{n}}(\bar{b}))^{-1}L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{\mathfrak{t}}(\bar{b}))^{-1}L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{\bar{{\mathfrak{n}}}}(\bar{b}))^{-1}.

By definition of the generic locus, the factor
L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{\mathfrak{n}}(\bar{b}))^{-1}L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{\bar{{\mathfrak{n}}}}(\bar{b}))^{-1}
is invertible. As
\sigma
is of finite index, the eigenvalues of the
\sigma
-action on
\mathfrak{t}
are roots of unity. Thus the factor
L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{\mathfrak{t}}(\bar{b}))^{-1}
is also invertible. Hence
\det(1-q^{-1}\mathrm{Ad}_{\mathfrak{g}}(b)\circ\sigma)
is invertible and we conclude that
n=0
.
∎

The following definition of the regular semisimple locus is a generalization of the usual definition of “regular semisimple” in the
\sigma=1
case.

Definition 2.2.3

(Regular semisimple locus)
.

We define the regular semisimple locus of
{\mathbb{L}}_{G\sigma}
,
{\mathbb{L}}_{B\sigma}
and
{\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma}
as follows.

•

For
T\sigma
, the regular semisimple locus
(T\sigma)^{rs}
is the non-vanishing locus of
L(0,\mathrm{Ad}_{\mathfrak{n}})^{-1}
and
{\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma}^{rs}
.

•

For
B\sigma
, we pull back the function
L(0,\mathrm{Ad}_{\mathfrak{n}})^{-1}
. Then the regular semisimple locus
(B\sigma)^{rs}
is the non-vanishing locus of
L(0,\mathrm{Ad}_{\mathfrak{n}})^{-1}
. Then
{\mathbb{L}}_{B\sigma}^{rs}
is defined to be the
open substack
\{(b\sigma,n)\mid b\sigma\in(B\sigma)^{rs}\}

of
{\mathbb{L}}_{B\sigma}
.

•

For
G\sigma
,
(G\sigma)^{rs}
the inverse image of
(T\sigma)^{rs}
under the twisted Chevalley map.
Then
{\mathbb{L}}_{G\sigma}^{rs}
is defined to be the
open substack
\{(g\sigma,n)\mid g\sigma\in(G\sigma)^{rs}\}

of
{\mathbb{L}}_{G\sigma}
.

Remark 2.2.4
.

By Lemma 5.2.14 of
[
17
]
,
the locus
(G\sigma)^{rs}
defined here is the same as that defined in Xiao–Zhu, where
(G\sigma)^{rs}
is defined to be the image of
G\times T\sigma\to G\sigma
, where
G
acts by conjugation.
Moreover, by Lemma 5.2.11 in
[
17
]
, the notion “regular semisimple” is the same as “regular and semisimple” in
G\sigma
.
Here the notion “regular” is defined in Definition
2.3.4

and an element in
G\sigma
is called semisimple if it is conjugate to an element in
T\sigma
, or equivalently saying, its corresponding L-parameter is semisimple.

By definition,
{\mathbb{L}}_{B\sigma}^{?}
is the inverse image of
{\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma}^{?}
under
q
, where ? is
rs
or
g
. We will see later that similar result holds for
p\colon{\mathbb{L}}_{B\sigma}\to{\mathbb{L}}_{G\sigma}
.

There is a slightly different notion about the regular semisimplicity.

Definition 2.2.5

(Strongly regular semisimple)
.

For
x\in(B\sigma)^{rs}/B\simeq(T\sigma)^{rs}/T
(the isomorphism is proved in
2.2.7
),
x
is called strongly regular semisimple if the subgroup of
W^{\sigma}
that fixes
x
is trivial.

The strongly regular semisimple
x
’s form an open subset of
T\sigma
, as
W^{\sigma}
is the Weyl group of
T_{\sigma}
.

To justify the definition of the generic locus and the regular semisimple locus, we may consider the cotangent complex.

Proposition 2.2.6

(The cotangent complex)
.

Denote by
i\colon{\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma}\to{\mathbb{L}}_{G\sigma}
the map induced by the natural inclusion. Then the cotangent complex
Li
satisfies the following:

•

The generic locus
{\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma}^{g}
is the open substack of
{\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma}
where
L^{1}i=0
.

•

The regular semisimple locus
{\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma}^{g}
is the open substack of
{\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma}
where
L^{-1}i=0
.

•

In particular, over
{\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma}^{rs,g}
, the cotangent complex vanishes and then
{\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma}^{rs,g}\to{\mathbb{L}}_{G\sigma}^{rs,g}
is étale.

Hence the regular semisimple and generic locus is the same as the open substack of parameters of “Langlands–Shahidi type” in the unipotent component.

Proof.

As mentioned above,
{\mathbb{L}}_{G\sigma}
is a connected component of
Z^{1}(W_{F},G)
. Thus by
[
5
]
, Proposition VIII.2.1, the cotangent complex of
{\mathbb{L}}_{G\sigma}
at a parameter
\phi\in Z^{1}(W_{F},G)
is

\phi^{*}L_{{\mathbb{L}}_{G\sigma}}\simeq R\Gamma(W_{F},\mathfrak{g}_{\phi})[1]

where the subscript
\phi
means twisting by
\phi
. Thus the cotangent complex
Li
at
\phi
is

\phi^{*}Li=\operatorname{cofib}(\phi^{*}L_{{\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma}}\to\phi^{*}L_{{\mathbb{L}}_{G\sigma}})=R\Gamma(W_{F},(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{t})_{\phi})[1]=R\Gamma(W_{F},({\mathfrak{n}}\oplus\bar{{\mathfrak{n}}})_{\phi}).

We have
H^{0}(W_{F},({\mathfrak{n}}\oplus\bar{{\mathfrak{n}}})_{\phi})
is the fixed points of
{\mathfrak{n}}\oplus\bar{{\mathfrak{n}}}
under
\mathrm{Ad}_{{\mathfrak{n}}\oplus\bar{{\mathfrak{n}}}}(t)\circ\sigma
if the parameter
\phi
corresponds to
(t,0)
in
{\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma}
. Thus the subset where
L^{-1}i=0
is the non-vanishing set of
L(0,\mathrm{Ad}_{{\mathfrak{n}}}\circ\sigma)^{-1}L(0,\mathrm{Ad}_{{\mathfrak{n}}}\circ\sigma)^{-1}
, which is the same as the regular semisimple locus (i.e., the non-vanishing set of
L(0,\mathrm{Ad}_{{\mathfrak{n}}}\circ\sigma)^{-1}
) by the concrete form of
L
-functions in
2.1.3
.

By
[
3
]
, Lemma 5.1,

H^{2}(W_{F},({\mathfrak{n}}\oplus\bar{{\mathfrak{n}}})_{\phi})^{*}\simeq H^{0}(W_{F},(({\mathfrak{n}}\oplus\bar{{\mathfrak{n}}})_{\phi})^{*}(1))

where
(1)
means the cyclotomic twist. Thus it is the fixed points of
({\mathfrak{n}}\oplus\bar{{\mathfrak{n}}})^{*}
under
q\mathrm{Ad}_{({\mathfrak{n}}\oplus\bar{{\mathfrak{n}}})^{*}}(t)\circ\sigma
. Thus the subset where
L^{1}i=0
is the non-vanishing set of
L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{{\mathfrak{n}}}\circ\sigma)^{-1}L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{{\mathfrak{n}}}\circ\sigma)^{-1}
, which is exactly the generic locus.

Again by
[
3
]
, Lemma 5.1, for a finitely dimensional
W_{F}
-representation
V
satisfying
H^{0}(W_{F},V)=H^{2}(W_{F},V)=0
, we have
R\Gamma(W_{F},V)=0
. Thus
Li=0
over the regular semisimple and generic locus and the morphism
{\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma}^{rs,g}\to{\mathbb{L}}^{rs_{g}}_{G\sigma}
is étale.
∎

Next we study the morphism
{\mathbb{L}}_{B\sigma}\to{\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma}
and
{\mathbb{L}}_{B\sigma}\to{\mathbb{L}}_{G\sigma}
.

Lemma 2.2.7
.

The pair of morphisms
\iota_{B}\colon(T\sigma)^{rs}/T\to(B\sigma)^{rs}/B
and
q_{0}\colon(B\sigma)^{rs}/B\to(T\sigma)^{rs}/T
(the morphisms are well-defined by definition of the regular semisimple locus) induced by the natural inclusion and projection provide an isomorphism
(B\sigma)^{rs}/B\simeq(T\sigma)^{rs}/T
and thus

{\mathbb{L}}_{B\sigma}^{rs,g}\simeq{\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma}^{rs,g}
.

Proof.

We have
q_{0}\circ\iota_{B}=\mathbb{1}
. By deformation theory in
2.2.6
,
\iota_{B}
is étale. Thus
\iota_{B}
is open and closed.
Moreover,
B
is connected. Thus
\iota_{B}
is an isomorphism.
The argument for
{\mathbb{L}}_{B\sigma}
follows from
2.2.2
.
∎

Lemma 2.2.8
.

The pair of morphisms
i\colon T\sigma\sslash T\to B\sigma\sslash B
and
q\colon B\sigma\sslash B\to T\sigma\sslash T
induced by the natural inclusion and projection provide an isomorphism
B\sigma\sslash B\simeq T\sigma\sslash T
.

Proof.

By Lemma
2.2.7
above,
(B\sigma)^{rs}\sslash B\simeq(T\sigma)^{rs}\sslash T
. We have
q^{*}\colon{\mathcal{O}}(T\sigma/T)\to{\mathcal{O}}(B\sigma/B)
is injective and splits as an
{\mathcal{O}}(T\sigma/T)
-module homomorphism. As
{\mathcal{O}}((T\sigma)^{rs}\sslash T)={\mathcal{O}}(T\sigma/T)[L(0,\mathrm{Ad}_{\mathfrak{n}})]
and similarly for
B
,
q^{*}
becomes an isomorphism after inverting
L(0,\mathrm{Ad}_{\mathfrak{n}})^{-1}
. Thus
q^{*}
is an isomorphism as
{\mathcal{O}}(T\sigma/T)
and
{\mathcal{O}}(B\sigma/B)
are integral domains.
∎

Thus we have the following factorization of the twisted Chevalley isomorphism.

Corollary 2.2.9
.

The morphism
T\sigma\sslash T\to G\sigma\sslash G
inducing the twisted Chevalley isomorphism (Proposition
2.1.5
) factors as

T\sigma\sslash T\xleftarrow[\sim]{q}B\sigma\sslash B\to G\sigma\sslash G.

From the corollary above,
{\mathbb{L}}_{B\sigma}^{?}
is the inverse image of
{\mathbb{L}}_{G\sigma}^{?}
under
q
, where ? is
rs
or
g
.
Hence the morphisms
{\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma}\leftarrow{\mathbb{L}}_{B\sigma}\to{\mathbb{L}}_{G\sigma}
restrict to their generic locus
{\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma}^{g}\leftarrow{\mathbb{L}}_{B\sigma}^{g}\to{\mathbb{L}}_{G\sigma}^{g}
and similarly for the regular semisimple locus. We will denote by
\operatorname{Eis}^{rs}
,
\operatorname{Eis}^{g}
,
\operatorname{Eis}^{rs,g}
the corresponding functors, similarly for
\operatorname{eis}
. Note that
\operatorname{Eis}^{?}\circ j_{T}^{?*}
is isomorphic to
j_{G}^{?*}\operatorname{Eis}
, where ? is rs or g or rs,g and
j_{T}^{?*}
,
j_{G}^{?*}
is the open immersion of the ?-locus of
{\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma}
and
{\mathbb{L}}_{G\sigma}
respectively. In the following notations, for
{\mathcal{F}}\in D\operatorname{Coh}({\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma})
, abusing the notations, we will abbreviate
\operatorname{Eis}^{?}j^{?*}_{T}{\mathcal{F}}
simply by
\operatorname{Eis}^{?}{\mathcal{F}}
.

Lemma 2.2.10

(
[
17
]
, Lemma 5.2.14)
.

The morphism
\iota_{G}\colon(T\sigma)^{rs}/T\to(G\sigma)^{rs}/G
induced by the natural inclusion
T\to G
induces an isomorphism

(T\sigma)^{rs}/N_{G}(T\sigma)\simeq(G\sigma)^{rs}/G
. Thus the morphism

(B\sigma)^{rs}/B\to(G\sigma)^{rs}/G
is a Galois covering and the same holds for

{\mathbb{L}}_{B\sigma}^{rs,g}\to{\mathbb{L}}_{G\sigma}^{rs,g}
.

Now we define the intertwining operator over the regular semisimple and generic locus.

Definition 2.2.11

(Intertwining over the regular semisimple and generic locus)
.

Consider the morphism
\iota_{G}\colon{\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma}^{rs,g}\to{\mathbb{L}}_{G\sigma}^{rs,g}
induced by
T\to G
and similarly for
\iota_{B}
. We define an isomorphism

t_{B}\colon\operatorname{Eis}_{B}^{rs,g}\simeq\iota_{G*}.

as

\operatorname{Eis}_{B}^{rs,g}=p_{B*}q_{B}^{*}\simeq p_{B^{*}}\iota_{B*}=\iota_{G*},

where the second isomorphism is deduced from the fact that
\iota_{B}
and
q_{B}
are mutually inverse and the third is from
\iota_{G}=p_{B}\circ\iota_{B}
.

For another Borel
B^{\prime}
of
G
containing
T
and stable under
\sigma
, we define the canonical isomorphism

J_{B^{\prime}|B}^{rs,g}\colon\operatorname{Eis}_{B}^{rs,g}\simeq\operatorname{Eis}_{B^{\prime}}^{rs,g}

as the composition of isomorphisms
t_{B^{\prime}}^{-1}t_{B}
, called the intertwining operator over the regular semisimple and generic locus.

In the same way, we obtain an isomorphism
J_{B^{\prime}|B,0}^{rs}\colon\operatorname{eis}_{B}^{rs}\simeq\operatorname{eis}_{B^{\prime}}^{rs}
.

We will sometimes denote
J^{rs,g}
by
J^{\circ}
.
The operators
J^{\circ}
satisfy the transitivity by definition.

Proposition 2.2.12
.

For any three Borel subgroups
B_{1},B_{2},B_{3}
of
G
fixed by
\sigma
and containing
T
,

J_{B_{3}|B_{2}}^{\circ}\circ J_{B_{2}|B_{1}}^{\circ}=J_{B_{3}|B_{1}}^{\circ}
.

Next we show that the isomorphism of functors
\operatorname{eis}_{B}^{rs}\simeq\operatorname{eis}_{B^{\prime}}^{rs}
above generates all transformations
\operatorname{eis}_{B}^{rs}\to\operatorname{eis}_{B^{\prime}}^{rs}
in some sense.

We introduce the following formalism. Let
F\colon{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{D}}
be a functor between additive categories. Then there is a natural ring homomorphism
{\mathcal{Z}}({\mathcal{C}}):=\operatorname{End}(\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{C}})\to\operatorname{End}(F)
. Moreover, let
G
be an another functor from
{\mathcal{C}}
to
{\mathcal{D}}
. Then
\operatorname{Hom}(F,G)
becomes a right
\operatorname{End}(F)
-module, and restricts to a right
{\mathcal{Z}}({\mathcal{C}})
-module.
We remark that we may also consider the
\operatorname{End}(G)
-action and deduce a
{\mathcal{Z}}({\mathcal{C}})
-module structure. These two module structure coincide.

Applying the above formalism to

{\mathcal{C}}=D\operatorname{Coh}({\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma})
,
{\mathcal{D}}=D\operatorname{Coh}({\mathbb{L}}_{G\sigma})
,
F=\operatorname{Eis}_{B}
,
G=\operatorname{Eis}_{B^{\prime}}
, we obtain a ring homomorphism
{\mathcal{Z}}(D\operatorname{Coh}({\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma}))\to\operatorname{End}(\operatorname{Eis}_{B})
and
\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{Eis}_{B},\operatorname{Eis}_{B^{\prime}})
is an
{\mathcal{Z}}(D\operatorname{Coh}({\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma}))
-module. All those constructions may apply over the regular semisimple or generic loci.

Now we state the proposition about the generator.

Proposition 2.2.13
.

The ring homomorphism
{\mathcal{Z}}(D\operatorname{Coh}({\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma}^{rs,g}))\to\operatorname{End}(\operatorname{Eis}^{rs,g})
is an isomorphism.
Moreover, the isomorphism
J_{B^{\prime}|B}^{rs,g}
in Definition
2.2.11
generates the free
{\mathcal{Z}}(D\operatorname{Coh}({\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma}^{rs,g}))
-module

\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{Eis}_{B}^{rs,g},\operatorname{Eis}_{B^{\prime}}^{rs,g})
. Similarly for
\operatorname{eis}^{rs}
.

In fact, we can prove the following general statement.

Proposition 2.2.14
.

Let
f\colon X\to Y
be a finite étale morphism between connected smooth algebraic stacks over
k
.
Suppose that there exists a closed point
x\in X

such that the isotropy groups of
x
and
y:=f(x)
are isomorphic.
Then applying the formalism above to
{\mathcal{C}}=D\operatorname{Coh}(X)
,
{\mathcal{D}}=D\operatorname{Coh}(Y)
and
F=f_{*}
, the homomorphism

{\mathcal{Z}}(D\operatorname{Coh}(X))\to\operatorname{End}(f_{*})

is an isomorphism.

Proof.

As
f
is étale,
f_{*}
is faithful and the ring homomorphism
{\mathcal{Z}}(D\operatorname{Coh}(X))\to\operatorname{End}(f_{*})
is injective. Then we prove that it is surjective. An element in
\operatorname{End}(f_{*})
is given by a compatible family of homomorphisms

\{a_{\mathcal{F}}\}_{{\mathcal{F}}\in\operatorname{Coh}(X)},\quad a_{\mathcal{F}}\in\operatorname{End}_{Y}(f_{*}{\mathcal{F}}).

As
X
is smooth and the characteristic of
k
is
0
, locally free sheaves generate the entire category
D\operatorname{Coh}(X)
. It suffices to prove that for each locally free sheaf
{\mathcal{F}}\in D\operatorname{Coh}(X)
,
a_{\mathcal{F}}\in f_{*}(\operatorname{End}_{X}({\mathcal{F}}))
.

In fact, consider the completion
{\mathcal{F}}_{x}^{\wedge}
(though it is not a coherent sheaf, we view it as an inverse system of coherent sheaves). It suffices to prove the following claim:

\operatorname{End}_{Y}(f_{*}{\mathcal{F}})\ \text{``}\cap\text{''}\ \operatorname{End}_{Y}(f_{*}{\mathcal{F}}_{x}^{\wedge})=f_{*}(\operatorname{End}_{X}({\mathcal{F}})),

i.e., if we have compatible homomorphisms
a^{\prime}_{\mathcal{F}}\in\operatorname{End}_{Y}(f_{*}{\mathcal{F}})

and
a^{\prime}_{{\mathcal{F}}_{x}^{\wedge}}\in\operatorname{End}_{Y}(f_{*}{\mathcal{F}}_{x}^{\wedge})
, then
a^{\prime}_{\mathcal{F}}\in f_{*}(\operatorname{End}_{X}({\mathcal{F}}))
.

By definition of algebraic stacks,
there exists a surjective smooth morphism
\tilde{Y}\to Y
such that
\tilde{Y}
and
\tilde{Y}\times_{Y}\tilde{Y}
are schemes.
Replacing
\tilde{Y}
by some Galois covering of it, we may assume that
f_{*}\colon\tilde{X}:=X\times_{Y}\tilde{Y}\to\tilde{Y}
is isomorphic to a disjoint union of some
\tilde{Y}
’s.
Over the smooth site over
Y
, the construction
S\mapsto\operatorname{End}_{S}({\mathcal{G}}_{S})
is sheafy for any
{\mathcal{G}}\in\operatorname{Coh}(Y)
. Here we use the subscript to denote the pullback sheaf on it. It suffices to prove that

\operatorname{End}_{\tilde{Y}}(\tilde{f}_{*}{\mathcal{F}}_{\tilde{X}})\ \text{``}\cap\text{''}\ \operatorname{End}_{\tilde{Y}}(\tilde{f}_{*}{\mathcal{F}}_{\tilde{X},\tilde{x}}^{\wedge})=\tilde{f}_{*}(\operatorname{End}_{\tilde{X}}({\mathcal{F}}_{\tilde{X}})),

where
\tilde{x}
the fiber of
x
in
\tilde{X}
.
By the condition about
x
, denoting by
\tilde{y}
the fiber of
y=f(x)
in
\tilde{Y}
,
\tilde{f}
induces an isomorphism
\tilde{x}\to\tilde{y}
. Write
\tilde{X}=\coprod\tilde{Y}_{i}
. We have

\operatorname{End}_{\tilde{Y}}(\tilde{f}_{*}{\mathcal{F}}_{\tilde{X}})=\operatorname{End}_{\tilde{Y}}(\bigoplus_{i}{\mathcal{F}}_{\tilde{Y}_{i}})

and

\operatorname{End}_{\tilde{Y}}(\tilde{f}_{*}{\mathcal{F}}_{\tilde{X},\tilde{x}}^{\wedge})=\operatorname{End}_{\tilde{Y}}(\bigoplus_{i}{\mathcal{F}}_{\tilde{Y_{i}},\tilde{x}\cap\tilde{Y}_{i}}^{\wedge})=\bigoplus_{i}\operatorname{End}_{\tilde{Y}}({\mathcal{F}}_{\tilde{Y_{i}},\tilde{x}\cap\tilde{Y}_{i}}^{\wedge}).

Here the last identity is from the following reasons:
As
\tilde{f}
induces an isomorphism
\tilde{x}\to\tilde{y}
,
\tilde{f}(\tilde{x}\cap\tilde{Y}_{i})
and
\tilde{f}(\tilde{x}\cap\tilde{Y}_{j})
are disjoint for different
i,j
. Thus the entries in the expression of
\operatorname{End}_{\tilde{Y}}(\tilde{f}_{*}{\mathcal{F}}_{\tilde{X},\tilde{x}}^{\wedge})
away from the diagonal must vanish.

Moreover, as
X
is connected and smooth, the homomorphism
{\mathcal{F}}\to{\mathcal{F}}_{x}^{\wedge}
is injective. As
\tilde{Y}_{i}\to X
is faithfully flat, for any
i
, its pullback on
\tilde{Y}_{i}

{\mathcal{F}}_{\tilde{Y}_{i}}\to{\mathcal{F}}_{\tilde{Y}_{i},\tilde{x}\cap\tilde{Y}_{i}}^{\wedge}

is injective. Thus we have

\operatorname{End}_{\tilde{Y}}(\tilde{f}_{*}{\mathcal{F}}_{\tilde{X}})\ \text{``}\cap\text{''}\ \operatorname{End}_{\tilde{Y}}(\tilde{f}_{*}{\mathcal{F}}_{\tilde{X},\tilde{x}}^{\wedge})=\bigoplus_{i}\operatorname{End}_{\tilde{Y}}({\mathcal{F}}_{\tilde{Y}_{i}})=\tilde{f}_{*}(\operatorname{End}_{\tilde{X}}({\mathcal{F}}_{\tilde{X}})).\qed

Now we turn to the proof of the original proposition.

Proof of Proposition
2.2.13
.

We prove the statement for
\operatorname{eis}^{rs}
and the statement for
\operatorname{Eis}^{rs,g}
is similar.
By Lemma
2.2.10
,
\iota_{G}\colon(T\sigma)^{rs}/T\to(G\sigma)^{rs}/G
is a Galois covering. For any point
x
in
T\sigma
, its stabilizer is
T^{\sigma}
and thus of the same dimension. Thus its image in
T\sigma/T
is closed. Moreover, for strongly regular semisimple
x
, the stabilizer of
x
under the
N_{G}(T\sigma)
-action is also
T^{\sigma}
. Thus
\iota_{G}
satisfies the conditions in Lemma
2.2.14
and then

{\mathcal{Z}}(D\operatorname{Coh}((T\sigma)^{rs}/T))\operatorname{\xrightarrow{\sim}}\operatorname{End}(\iota_{G*})\simeq\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{eis}_{B}^{rs},\operatorname{eis}_{B^{\prime}}^{rs})

As the intertwining
\operatorname{eis}_{B}^{rs}\simeq\operatorname{eis}_{B^{\prime}}^{rs}
is defined by
\operatorname{eis}_{B}^{rs}\simeq\iota_{G*}\simeq\operatorname{eis}_{B^{\prime}}^{rs}
in Definition
2.2.11
,
the isomorphism
J_{B^{\prime}|B,0}^{rs}\colon\operatorname{eis}_{B}^{rs}\simeq\operatorname{eis}_{B^{\prime}}^{rs}
in
2.2.11
corresponds to
1
in the left hand side and thus it is a generator.
∎

Next we analyze the structure of
T\sigma/T
and the category
D\operatorname{Coh}(T\sigma/T)
.

Lemma 2.2.15
.

The morphism
\rho\colon T\sigma/T\to T\sigma\sslash T=T_{\sigma}

is a
T^{\sigma}
-gerbe and
it has a (non-canonical) splitting
T\sigma/T\simeq T_{\sigma}\times*/T^{\sigma}
.

Proof.

We prove the following general argument: For a homomorphism of tori
\psi\colon T_{1}\to T_{2}
,
T_{2}/T_{1}\simeq T_{2}/\operatorname{im}(\psi)\times*/\ker(\psi)
(the argument in the lemma is the special case
T_{1}=T_{2}=T
and
\psi=1-\sigma
). In this case,
\operatorname{im}(\psi)
is again a torus. We claim that there exists a subtorus
T_{3}
of
T_{2}
such that
T_{2}=T_{3}\times\operatorname{im}(\psi)
. In fact, the inclusion
\operatorname{im}(\psi)\subset T_{2}
induces a surjection
X^{*}(T_{2})\to X^{*}(\operatorname{im}(\psi))
and we have a splitting
i
of the surjection. Then we may take
T_{3}
as the subtorus corresponding to the quotient
X^{*}(T_{2})/\operatorname{im}(i)
of
X^{*}(T_{2})
.

Then we have

T_{2}/T_{1}=(T_{3}\times\operatorname{im}(\psi))/T_{1}\simeq T_{3}\times\operatorname{im}(\psi)/T_{1}\simeq T_{2}/\operatorname{im}(\psi)\times*/\ker(\psi).\qed

Thus there is a block decomposition of
D\operatorname{Coh}({\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma})
, according to the character of
T^{\sigma}
:

D\operatorname{Coh}({\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma})\simeq\bigoplus_{\chi\in X^{*}(T^{\sigma})}D\operatorname{Coh}({\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma})_{\chi}.

The summand
D\operatorname{Coh}({\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma})_{1}
of this decomposition is equivalent to the category
D\operatorname{Coh}(T_{\sigma})
under
\rho^{*}
.
We remark that the decomposition is independent of the trivialization.

Moreover, denote by
{\mathcal{O}}_{\chi}
the invertible sheaf
{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}}\boxtimes k_{\chi}
on
T\sigma/T
(it depends on the choice of the trivialization a priori).
Then
{\mathcal{F}}\mapsto{\mathcal{F}}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{\chi}
defines an equivalence of categories

D\operatorname{Coh}({\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma})_{1}\simeq D\operatorname{Coh}({\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma})_{\chi}.

Since
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma}}
generates the subcategory
D\operatorname{Coh}({\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma})_{1}
, the sheaf
{\mathcal{O}}_{\chi}
generates the subcategory
D\operatorname{Coh}({\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma})_{\chi}
.
Hence the Bernstein center of such a subcategory is

{\mathcal{Z}}(D\operatorname{Coh}({\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma})_{\chi})\simeq\operatorname{End}({\mathcal{O}}_{\chi})\simeq{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma}).

Applying the block decomposition to Proposition
2.2.13
,
we see that the intertwining operator for each subcategory is also a generator:

Proposition 2.2.16
.

For any character
\chi
of
T^{\sigma}
,
the ring homomorphism

{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma}^{rs,g})={\mathcal{Z}}(D\operatorname{Coh}({\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma}^{rs,g})_{\chi})\to\operatorname{End}(\operatorname{Eis}^{rs,g})

is an isomorphism.
Here
\operatorname{Eis}
is restricted to the subcategory
{\mathcal{Z}}(D\operatorname{Coh}({\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma}^{rs,g})_{\chi})
.
Moreover, the isomorphism
J_{B^{\prime}|B}^{rs,g}
in
2.2.11
generates the free
{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma}^{rs,g})
-module

\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{Eis}_{B}^{rs,g},\operatorname{Eis}_{B^{\prime}}^{rs,g})
. Similarly for
\operatorname{eis}^{rs}
.

2.3
Extending to the generic locus

We will extend the isomorphism in Definition
2.2.11
to the generic locus.
More precisely, in this subsection we will prove the following statement.

Proposition 2.3.1

(Intertwining over the generic locus)
.

Let
\chi
be a character of
T^{\sigma}
that is invariant under the
W^{\sigma}
-action.
Denote by
\operatorname{Eis}^{g}
the restriction of the spectral Eisenstein series functor to the generic locus.
The isomorphism constructed in
2.2.11
,
J_{B^{\prime}|B}^{rs,g}\colon\operatorname{Eis}_{B}^{rs,g}\simeq\operatorname{Eis}_{B^{\prime}}^{rs,g}
can be uniquely extended to the generic locus

J_{B^{\prime}|B}^{g}\colon\operatorname{Eis}_{B}^{g}\simeq\operatorname{Eis}_{B^{\prime}}^{g}
,
after restricting to the category of sheaves

D\operatorname{Coh}({\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma})_{\chi}
.
Moreover, the
{\mathcal{O}}({\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma}^{g})
-module

\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{Eis}_{B}^{g},\operatorname{Eis}_{B^{\prime}}^{g})
is free and
J^{g}_{B^{\prime}|B}
is a generator of it.

In the same way, the isomorphism
J^{rs}_{B^{\prime}|B,0}\colon\operatorname{eis}_{B}^{rs}\simeq\operatorname{eis}_{B^{\prime}}^{rs}
in
2.2.11
can be extended uniquely to an isomorphism
J_{B^{\prime}|B,0}\colon\operatorname{eis}_{B}\simeq\operatorname{eis}_{B^{\prime}}
after restricted to
D\operatorname{Coh}(T\sigma/T)_{\chi}
and the resulting isomorphism generates the free
{\mathcal{O}}(T\sigma/T)
-module

\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{eis}_{B},\operatorname{eis}_{B^{\prime}})
.

In the following proofs, we will consider the functor
\operatorname{eis}
only. The statement about
\operatorname{Eis}^{g}
follows directly from the isomorphism
{\mathbb{L}}_{G\sigma}^{g}\simeq(G\sigma)^{g}/G
.

We remark that the condition that
\chi\in X^{*}(T^{\sigma})^{W_{\sigma}}
is equivalent to that after restricting to the neutral component
(T^{\sigma})^{\circ}
of
T^{\sigma}
,
\chi|_{(T^{\sigma})^{\circ}}
is
W^{\sigma}
-invariant. In fact, we have the following lemma.

Lemma 2.3.2
.

Denote by
Z(G)
the center of
G
. Then the homomorphism
Z(G)^{\sigma}\to\pi_{0}(T^{\sigma})
is surjective. In particular,
W^{\sigma}
acts trivially on
\pi_{0}(T^{\sigma})
.

Proof.

It suffices to prove that the homomorphism of characters
(X^{*}(T^{\sigma}))_{\text{tor}}\to X^{*}(Z(G)^{\sigma})
is injective, where ”tor” denotes the torsion part. For any multiplicative group
S
, we have
X^{*}(S^{\sigma})\simeq X^{*}(S)_{\sigma}
.
Moreover,

X^{*}(Z(G))\simeq X^{*}(T)/Q
, where
Q
denotes the root lattice.
Thus from the exact sequence

0\to Q\to X^{*}(T)\to X^{*}(T)/Q\to 0,

we have a right exact sequence

Q_{\sigma}\to X^{*}(T)_{\sigma}\to(X^{*}(T)/Q)_{\sigma}\to 0.

As
\sigma
acts by permutation of the roots,
Q_{\sigma}
is free. Thus
(X^{*}(T)_{\sigma})_{\text{tor}}\to(X^{*}(T)/Q)_{\sigma}
is injective and we conclude.
∎

In particular, it
\chi
is trivial on
(T^{\sigma})^{\circ}
, it is
W^{\sigma}
-invariant.

It is worth noting that the requirement that
\chi
is
W^{\sigma}
-invariant is necessary.

Remark 2.3.3
.

The sheaves
\operatorname{eis}_{B}{\mathcal{F}}
and
\operatorname{eis}_{B^{\prime}}{\mathcal{F}}
are not isomorphic for general
{\mathcal{F}}
in
D\operatorname{Coh}(T\sigma/T)
. For example, let
B^{\prime}=w(B)
and
{\mathcal{F}}=i_{1*}{\mathcal{O}}_{\lambda}
, the skyscraper sheaf at
1\in T
for a weight
\lambda
of
T
. Then the fiber of
\operatorname{eis}_{B}{\mathcal{F}}
and
\operatorname{eis}_{B^{\prime}}{\mathcal{F}}
at
1\in G
is
R\Gamma(G^{\sigma}/B^{\sigma},{\mathcal{O}}_{\lambda})
and
R\Gamma(G^{\sigma}/B^{\sigma},{\mathcal{O}}_{w\lambda})
respectively. By Borel–Weil–Bott theorem, if they are isomorphic for any
w\in W^{\sigma}
,
\lambda
must be invariant under
W^{\sigma}
-action.

For the proof of the main proposition,
we consider the following auxiliary definition of regular locus.
In
[
17
]
, the regular locus is defined as follows.

Definition 2.3.4

(Regular locus)
.

The regular locus
(G\sigma)^{r}
is defined as

(G\sigma)^{r}:=\{g\sigma\in G\sigma\mid\dim C_{G}(g\sigma)=\dim T^{\sigma}\}

where
C_{G}(g\sigma)
is the twisted centralizer

C_{G}(g\sigma):=\{h\in G\mid h(g\sigma)h^{-1}=g\sigma\}=\{h\in G\mid hg\sigma(h)^{-1}=g\}.

The regular locus
(B\sigma)^{r}
is defined as
(B\sigma)^{r}:=(G\sigma)^{r}\cap B
.

Note that when
\sigma=1
, it is the usual definition of regular locus.
We have the following lemma about the complement of
(B\sigma)^{r}
and
(G\sigma)^{r}
.

Lemma 2.3.5

(
[
17
]
, corollary 5.2.21)
.

The complement of the regular locus is of codimension at least
2
:

\operatorname{codim}(B\sigma-(B\sigma)^{r})\geq 2
in
B
and

\operatorname{codim}(G\sigma-(G\sigma)^{r})\geq 2
in
G
.

We have the following generalization of the Kostant’s theorem.

Proposition 2.3.6
.

Let
G
be as above. Suppose that
G_{0}\to G
is a covering such that
G_{0}
is a product of a semisimple and simply connected group and a torus and the action
\sigma
on
G
can be lifted to a compatible action on
G_{0}
, which is denoted also by
\sigma
.
(Note that if such a lifting exists, then it is unique. Moreover, fixing
G
,
we have
G_{0}=G^{sc}\times Z(G)^{\circ}
satisfies this condition, where
G^{sc}
is the universal cover of the derived subgroup of
G
and
Z(G)^{\circ}
is neutral component of the center of
G
.) Denote by
T_{0}
the inverse image of
T
in
G_{0}
and similarly for
B_{0}
. We have
G_{0}\to G
is a Galois covering with Galois group
\ker(G_{0}\to G)=\ker(B_{0}\to B)=\ker(T_{0}\to T)
.

Then the squares in the commutative diagram are cartesian:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{G\sigma}^{r}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(B\sigma)^{r}/B_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(T_{0}\sigma\sslash T_{0})/\operatorname{Gal}(G_{0}/G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\sigma\sslash T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(G\sigma)^{r}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(G\sigma)^{r}/G_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(T_{0}\sigma\sslash N_{G_{0}}(T_{0}\sigma))/\operatorname{Gal}(G_{0}/G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\sigma\sslash^{\prime}N_{G}(T\sigma),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pi^{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{B}^{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{B}^{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{r}}

where
\widetilde{G\sigma}
is the twisted Grothendieck–Springer resolution

\widetilde{G\sigma}:=\{(g\sigma,h)\in G\sigma\times G/B\mid h^{-1}g\sigma(h)\in B\},

\widetilde{G\sigma}^{r}
is the inverse image of
(G\sigma)^{r}
,

T\sigma\sslash^{\prime}N_{G}(T\sigma)
is

(T_{0}\sigma\sslash N_{G_{0}}(T_{0}\sigma))/\ker(T_{0,\sigma}\to T_{\sigma})

and
(G\sigma)^{r}/G_{0}\to(T_{0}\sigma\sslash N_{G_{0}}(T_{0}\sigma))/\operatorname{Gal}(G_{0}/G)
is induced by the twisted Chevalley morphism for
G_{0}
.

We will call
G_{0}\to G
to be a simply connected cover of
G
if it satisfies the conditions in this proposition.

Proof.

We firstly assume that
G_{0}\to G
is an isomorphism.
The case that
G_{0}
is semisimple is proved by Xiao and Zhu
(
[
17
]
, Proposition 5.3.1). The case that
G
is a torus is trivial as
G=B=T
in this case.
In the case that
G
is the product of a semisimple and simply connected subgroup and a torus,
any construction in the diagram about
G
is the product of such construction on the simply connected subgroup and such construction on the torus and the result holds.

In general, we have a commutative diagram for
G_{0}
by discussions above. As all constructions except the two right terms in this diagram are obtained by quotienting out
\operatorname{Gal}(G_{0}/G)
on those constructions about
G_{0}
, it suffices to prove that the rightmost square is cartesian. Note that
\operatorname{Gal}(G_{0}/G)=\ker(G_{0}\to G)=\ker(T_{0}\to T)
acts on
T_{0,\sigma}
by multiplication. We have

T_{\sigma}\simeq T_{0,\sigma}\sslash\operatorname{Gal}(G_{0}/G)\simeq T_{0,\sigma}/\operatorname{im}(\operatorname{Gal}(G_{0}/G)\to T_{0,\sigma})=T_{0,\sigma}/\ker(T_{0,\sigma}\to T_{\sigma})

and thus the rightmost square is cartesian.
∎

Remark 2.3.7
.

The space
T\sigma\sslash^{\prime}N_{G}(T\sigma)
is independent of the choice of
G_{0}
.
The proof of this statement is as follows.
Let
G_{0}\to G
be as above. Then
G_{0}
is of the form
G^{sc}\times\tilde{Z}
with a covering
\tilde{Z}\to Z(G)^{\circ}
.
Denote by
G_{1}:=G^{sc}\times Z(G)^{\circ}
and
T_{1}
its torus lifting
T
.
By definition of
T\sigma\sslash^{\prime}N_{G}(T\sigma)
for
G_{0}
, it is

(T_{0}\sigma\sslash N_{G_{0}}(T_{0}\sigma))/\ker(T_{0,\sigma}\to T_{\sigma})\simeq((T_{0}\sigma\sslash N_{G_{0}}(T_{0}\sigma))/\ker(T_{0,\sigma}\to T_{1,\sigma}))/\ker(T_{1,\sigma}\to T_{\sigma}).

Moreover, as the construction
T_{0}\sigma\sslash N_{G_{0}}(T_{0}\sigma)
about
G_{0}
is the product of such construction about
G^{sc}
and

\tilde{Z}
and similarly for
G_{1}
, we have

(T_{0}\sigma\sslash N_{G_{0}}(T_{0}\sigma))/\ker(T_{0,\sigma}\to T_{1,\sigma})\simeq(T_{0}\sigma\sslash N_{G_{0}}(T_{0}\sigma))/\ker(\tilde{Z}_{\sigma}\to Z(G)^{\circ}_{\sigma})\simeq T_{1}\sigma\sslash N_{G_{1}}(T_{1}\sigma).

Thus
T\sigma\sslash^{\prime}N_{G}(T\sigma)
defined via
G_{0}
and
G_{1}
are isomorphic.

Let
G_{0}\to G
be a simply connected cover.
Denote by
\beta
the
\operatorname{Gal}(G_{0}/G)
-gerbe
G\sigma/G_{0}\to G\sigma/G
.
Then
\beta^{*}
induces an equivalence of categories

D\operatorname{Coh}(G\sigma/G)\xrightarrow{\sim}D\operatorname{Coh}(G\sigma/G_{0})_{1,\Gamma},

where the latter denotes the subcategory of
D\operatorname{Coh}(G\sigma/G_{0})
that consists of sheaves with trivial
\Gamma=\operatorname{Gal}(G_{0}/G)
-action. Denote by
q_{B}^{r}\colon(B\sigma)^{r}/B_{0}\to T_{\sigma}
and
\pi_{T}^{r}\colon(G\sigma)^{r}/G_{0}\to T\sigma\sslash^{\prime}N_{G}(T\sigma)
. By definition of
\operatorname{eis}_{B}
and proper base change in the diagram in Proposition
2.3.6
, we have

\beta^{*}\operatorname{eis}_{B}^{r}\simeq p_{B*}^{r}q_{B}^{r*}\simeq\pi_{T}^{r*}p_{T*}.

To prove Proposition
2.3.1
, we firstly consider the block
D\operatorname{Coh}(T\sigma/T)_{1}
.
By Lemma
2.2.15
,
\rho^{*}
induces an equivalence of categories

D\operatorname{Coh}(T\sigma\sslash T)\simeq D\operatorname{Coh}(T\sigma/T)_{1}.

We will then identify these two categories if there is no ambiguity.

The following proposition is about the coherent Springer sheaf, which is a generalization of a classical result in Springer theory,

Proposition 2.3.8
.

\operatorname{eis}_{B}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}}=(p_{B})_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{B\sigma/B}
is an honest coherent sheaf on
G\sigma/G
.

Proof.

In the case
\sigma=1
, it is proved in
[
11
]
, Theorem B. In general, we have not found a reference which contains a simple proof of the argument and we refer to the article of Zhu about the categorical local Langlands correspondence.
In
[
19
]
, Corollary 5.11, it is proved that
\operatorname{Eis}_{B}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}}
is an honest coherent sheaf. As
\operatorname{Eis}_{B}^{g}\simeq\operatorname{eis}_{B}^{g}
,
(p_{B})_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{B\sigma/B}
is an honest coherent sheaf on
(G\sigma)^{g}/G
. We may change the parameter
q=p^{n}
in the definition of
{\mathbb{L}}_{G\sigma}
and conclude that
\operatorname{eis}_{B}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}}
is an honest coherent sheaf on
G^{g,q}
, the generic locus for
q
. For all such
q
, the union of
G^{g,q}
is
G
. Thus
\operatorname{eis}_{B}{\mathcal{O}}_{T}
is an honest coherent sheaf.
∎

Lemma 2.3.9
.

Let
G_{0}\to G
be a covering as in Proposition
2.3.6
.
Consider the following diagram, extending the diagram above:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{G\sigma}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\sigma/B_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\sigma\sslash T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\sigma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\sigma/G_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\sigma\sslash^{\prime}N_{G}(T\sigma).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{T}}

(1)

Then the isomorphism
\beta^{*}\operatorname{eis}_{B}^{r}\simeq\pi_{T}^{r*}p_{T*}
above extends uniquely to
\beta^{*}\operatorname{eis}_{B}\simeq\pi_{T}^{*}p_{T*}
. Moreover,
\operatorname{eis}_{B}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}}
is locally free.

Proof.

In this proof, we will denote
j_{X}
the open immersion
X^{r}\to X
where
X
appears in the above diagram.

It suffices to show that after pulling back to
G\sigma
via
\pi
, the isomorphism extends uniquely to the whole
G\sigma
and
\pi^{*}\beta^{*}\operatorname{eis}_{B}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}}
is locally free. We have

\pi^{*}\beta^{*}\operatorname{eis}_{B}{\mathcal{O}}_{T\sigma/T}\simeq\pi^{*}(p_{B})_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{B\sigma/B}\simeq(P_{B})_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{G\sigma}}.

As
\widetilde{G\sigma}-\widetilde{G\sigma}^{r}
is of codimension at least
2
,
{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{G\sigma}}\simeq R^{0}j_{\widetilde{G\sigma}*}{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{G\sigma}^{r}}
by
[
15
]
, tag 0EBJ about the unique extension of reflexive coherent sheaves. By Proposition
2.3.8
, the higher cohomology of
(P_{B})_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{\tilde{G}_{\sigma}}

vanishes.
Thus
(P_{B})_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{G\sigma}}\simeq R^{0}j_{G*}(P_{B}^{r})_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{G\sigma}^{r}}
.
Then by Proposition
2.3.6
and proper base change,

(P_{B})_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{G\sigma}}\simeq R^{0}j_{G*}j^{*}_{G}(\pi_{T}\pi)^{*}p_{T*}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}}.

As
p_{T}
is finite and flat,
(\pi_{T}\pi)^{*}p_{T*}{\mathcal{O}}_{T\sslash_{\sigma}T}
is locally free over
G\sigma
and again by
[
15
]
, tag 0EBJ we have

R^{0}j_{G*}j^{*}_{G}(\pi_{T}\pi)^{*}p_{T*}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}}\simeq(\pi_{T}\pi)^{*}p_{T*}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}}.

Thus

\pi^{*}\beta^{*}\operatorname{eis}_{B}{\mathcal{O}}_{T\sigma/T}\simeq(\pi_{T}\pi)^{*}p_{T*}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}}

and
\operatorname{eis}_{B}{\mathcal{O}}_{T\sigma/T}
is a locally free
{\mathcal{O}}_{G\sigma/G}
-module. From its construction, this isomorphism extends the isomorphism
\beta^{*}\operatorname{eis}^{r}_{B}\simeq\pi_{T}^{r*}p_{T*}
over the regular locus applying to
{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}}
.

Recall that
{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}}
generates of the category
D\operatorname{Coh}(T_{\sigma})
.
The extension of the isomorphism is unique as the regular locus in
G\sigma
is open and dense and
\pi^{*}\operatorname{eis}_{B}{\mathcal{O}}_{T\sigma/T}
is locally free.
The construction is
{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}}
-linear by uniqueness of the extension and by linearity we may induce an isomorphism of functors
\beta^{*}\operatorname{eis}_{B}\simeq\pi_{T}^{*}p_{T*}
.
∎

Now we can conclude the proof of the main proposition for
\chi=1
.

Proof of Proposition
2.3.1
for
\chi=1
.

We prove the arguments about
\operatorname{eis}_{B}\simeq\operatorname{eis}_{B^{\prime}}
and then the arguments about
\operatorname{Eis}^{g}_{B}\simeq\operatorname{Eis}^{g}_{B^{\prime}}
follow in the same way.

Take a covering
G_{0}\to G
such that
\sigma
can be lifted to
G_{0}
and
G_{0}
is a product of a simply connected semisimple group and a torus.
Then there is an isomorphism
\beta^{*}\operatorname{eis}_{B}\operatorname{\xrightarrow{\sim}}\beta^{*}\operatorname{eis}_{B^{\prime}}
via the composition

\beta^{*}\operatorname{eis}_{B}\operatorname{\xrightarrow{\sim}}\pi_{T}^{*}p_{T*}\operatorname{\xrightarrow{\sim}}\beta^{*}\operatorname{eis}_{B^{\prime}}

in Lemma
2.3.9
. As
\beta^{*}
is fully faithful, this isomorphism induces an isomorphism
\operatorname{eis}_{B}\operatorname{\xrightarrow{\sim}}\operatorname{eis}_{B^{\prime}}
and we define
J_{B^{\prime}|B}^{g}
to be this isomorphism.

As we have
(G\sigma)^{rs}/G\simeq(T\sigma)^{rs}/N_{G}(T\sigma)
via the Chevalley map, we have
\beta^{*}\iota_{G*}\simeq\pi_{T}^{*}p_{T*}
over the regular semisimple locus. Thus by definition of the intertwining operator
J_{B^{\prime}|B}^{\circ}
in Definition
2.2.11
,

J_{B^{\prime}|B}^{g}
extends
J_{B^{\prime}|B}^{\circ}
.

For the uniqueness of the extension, it suffices to prove that the restriction

\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{eis}_{B},\operatorname{eis}_{B^{\prime}})\to\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{eis}_{B}^{rs},\operatorname{eis}_{B^{\prime}}^{rs})

is injective. As
{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}}
is a generator of
D\operatorname{Coh}(T_{\sigma})
,

\operatorname{eis}_{B}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}}
and
\operatorname{eis}_{B^{\prime}}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}}
are locally free by Lemma
2.3.9
and the regular semisimple locus is open and dense in
G\sigma
. The statement holds.

For the statement about the generator, notice that

\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{eis}_{B},\operatorname{eis}_{B^{\prime}})\subset\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{eis}_{B}^{rs},\operatorname{eis}_{B^{\prime}}^{rs})\simeq{\mathcal{O}}({\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma}^{rs,g})\simeq{\mathcal{O}}({\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma}^{g})[L(0,\mathrm{Ad}_{\mathfrak{n}})].

Thus if the isomorphism
J_{B^{\prime}|B}^{g}
were not a generator, it would be divisible by some nontrivial factor of
L(0,\mathrm{Ad}_{\mathfrak{n}})^{-1}
. But this is impossible, as
J^{g}_{B^{\prime}|B}({\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}})
defines an isomorphism of locally free
G\sigma/G
-modules and it cannot be divisible by such factor.
∎

Next we consider the case for general
\chi
.

Lemma 2.3.10
.

If
G
is semisimple and simply connected,
X^{*}(T^{\sigma})^{W^{\sigma}}=1
.

Proof.

As
G
is semisimple and simply connected,
G^{\sigma}
is a connected reductive group with Weyl group
W^{\sigma}
by
[
16
]
, Theorem 8.1. Thus the abelianization
(G^{\sigma})_{\text{ab}}
of
G^{\sigma}
is trivial and

X^{*}(T^{\sigma})^{W^{\sigma}}\simeq X^{*}((G^{\sigma})_{\text{ab}})=1.

∎

Lemma 2.3.11
.

For any
\chi\in X^{*}(T^{\sigma})^{W^{\sigma}}
, the sheaf
\operatorname{eis}_{B}{\mathcal{O}}_{\chi}
is locally free (and in particular concentrated in degree
0
).

Proof.

Choose a simply connected cover
G_{0}\to G
. It induces a morphism
\alpha_{G}\colon G_{0}\sigma/G_{0}\to G\sigma/G
, which is a composition of a Galois covering
G_{0}\sigma/G_{0}\to G\sigma/G_{0}
and a gerbe
G\sigma/G_{0}\to G\sigma/G
. Thus it suffices to prove that
\alpha_{G}^{*}\operatorname{eis}_{B}{\mathcal{O}}_{\chi}
is locally free.

Denote by
\alpha_{T}
the morphism
T_{0}\sigma/T_{0}\to T\sigma/T
.
By definition of
\operatorname{eis}_{B}
and proper base change,

\alpha_{G}^{*}\operatorname{eis}_{B}{\mathcal{O}}_{\chi}\simeq\operatorname{eis}_{B_{0}}\alpha_{T}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{\chi}\simeq\operatorname{eis}_{B_{0}}{\mathcal{O}}_{\tilde{\chi}},

where
\tilde{\chi}
is the pullback of
\chi
along
T_{0,\sigma}\to T_{\sigma}
. Hence it suffices to prove the statement for
G_{0}
.

By definition of
G_{0}
, it is a product of a torus and a semisimple and simply connected group. As the construction of
\operatorname{eis}
is compatible with direct product and the statement for a torus is trivial (since it is isomorphic to its Borel and its torus), it remains to prove the case that
G
is a semisimple and simply connected group.

By lemma
2.3.10
,
\chi=1
in this case.
Thus this lemma is reduced to Lemma
2.3.9
and we conclude.
∎

Lemma 2.3.12
.

For
\chi\in X^{*}(T^{\sigma})^{W^{\sigma}}
,

{\mathcal{O}}_{\chi}
can be descended to an invertible sheaf
\overline{{\mathcal{O}}_{\chi}}
over
T\sigma/N_{G}(T\sigma)
.
In particular, over the regular and semisimple locus
(T\sigma)^{rs}/T
,
{\mathcal{O}}_{\chi}^{rs}
can be descended to an invertible sheaf
\overline{{\mathcal{O}}_{\chi}^{rs}}
over
(G\sigma)^{rs}/G\simeq(T\sigma)^{rs}/N_{G}(T\sigma)
.

Proof.

Let
G_{0}=Z\times G^{sc}\to G
be a simply connected cover of
G
.
Then we have a covering
T_{0}=Z\times T^{sc}\to T
. Denote by
\Gamma
the kernel of the covering. Consider the following commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{0}\sigma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\sigma/T_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\sigma/T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\sigma/N_{G_{0}}(T_{0}\sigma)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\sigma/N_{G}(T\sigma)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{T}}

By Lemma
2.3.10
,

\chi|_{(T^{sc})^{\sigma}}
is trivial.
Thus for
\tilde{\alpha}_{T}\colon T_{0}\sigma\to T\sigma/T
, we have a
T_{0}\times\Gamma
-equivariant sheaf on
T_{0}\sigma
,

\tilde{\alpha}_{T}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{\chi}\simeq{\mathcal{O}}_{\chi|_{Z\sigma}}\boxtimes{\mathcal{O}}_{T^{sc}\sigma},

where
T^{sc}
acts trivially on
{\mathcal{O}}_{T^{sc}\sigma}
.
As
N_{G_{0}}(T_{0}\sigma)=Z\times N_{G^{sc}}(T^{sc}\sigma)
,
the sheaf
{\mathcal{O}}_{\chi|_{Z\sigma}}\boxtimes{\mathcal{O}}_{T^{sc}}
on
T_{0}\sigma
has a
\Gamma\times N_{G_{0}}(T_{0}\sigma)
-equivariant structure extending the equivariant structure above, such that
N_{G^{sc}}(T^{sc}\sigma)
acts trivially on it.

Thus the sheaf above descends to a sheaf (denoted by
{\mathcal{O}}_{\chi}^{\prime}
) on the stack

T_{0}\sigma/(\Gamma\times N_{G_{0}}(T_{0}\sigma))\simeq T\sigma/N_{G_{0}}(T_{0}\sigma).

The morphism
T\sigma/N_{G_{0}}(T_{0}\sigma)\to T\sigma/N_{G}(T\sigma)
is a
\Gamma
-gerbe. By construction,
\gamma_{T}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{\chi}^{\prime}\simeq\beta_{T}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{\chi}
. Thus the
\Gamma
-action from the
\Gamma
-gerbe structure of
\beta_{T}
on any fiber of
\gamma_{T}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{\chi}^{\prime}
is trivial.
Hence the
\Gamma
-action on any fiber of
{\mathcal{O}}_{\chi}^{\prime}
is trivial. Thus
{\mathcal{O}}_{\chi}^{\prime}
comes from a sheaf on
T\sigma/N_{G}(T\sigma)
as we desired.
∎

Recall that for any
\chi\in X^{*}(T^{\sigma})
,
the automorphism of
D\operatorname{Coh}(T\sigma/T)
given by

{\mathcal{F}}\mapsto{\mathcal{F}}_{\chi}:={\mathcal{F}}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{\chi}

induces an equivalence of categories

D\operatorname{Coh}(T\sigma/T)_{1}\operatorname{\xrightarrow{\sim}}D\operatorname{Coh}(T\sigma/T)_{\chi}.

By the projection formula, we have for any
{\mathcal{F}}\in D\operatorname{Coh}(T\sigma/T)
,

\operatorname{eis}_{B}^{rs}{\mathcal{F}}_{\chi}\simeq\operatorname{eis}_{B}^{rs}{\mathcal{F}}\otimes\overline{{\mathcal{O}}_{\chi}^{rs}}.

Moreover, tensoring by
\overline{{\mathcal{O}}_{\chi}^{rs}}
defines an isomorphism
(which will be denoted by
(-)\otimes\overline{{\mathcal{O}}_{\chi}}

\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{eis}_{B}^{rs}{\mathcal{F}},\operatorname{eis}_{B^{\prime}}^{rs}{\mathcal{G}})\operatorname{\xrightarrow{\sim}}\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{eis}_{B}^{rs}{\mathcal{F}}_{\chi},\operatorname{eis}_{B^{\prime}}^{rs}{\mathcal{G}}_{\chi})

(2)

For a locally free sheaf
{\mathcal{F}}\in D\operatorname{Coh}(T\sigma/T)_{\chi}
where
\chi
is invariant by
W^{\sigma}
,
\operatorname{eis}_{B}{\mathcal{F}}
is locally free by Lemma
2.3.11
and thus
\operatorname{eis}_{B}{\mathcal{F}}\to\operatorname{eis}_{B}^{rs}{\mathcal{F}}
is injective.

Lemma 2.3.13
.

For locally free sheaves
{\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}}\in D\operatorname{Coh}(T\sigma/T)_{1}
,
the isomorphism
(-)\otimes\overline{{\mathcal{O}}_{\chi}}
in
2
restricts to an isomorphism

\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{eis}_{B}{\mathcal{F}},\operatorname{eis}_{B^{\prime}}{\mathcal{G}})\operatorname{\xrightarrow{\sim}}\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{eis}_{B}{\mathcal{F}}_{\chi},\operatorname{eis}_{B^{\prime}}{\mathcal{G}}_{\chi}).

Moreover, this isomorphism extends to all
{\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}}\in D\operatorname{Coh}(T\sigma/T)_{1}
.

Proof.

For the statement about locally free sheaves,
it suffices to prove the statement after pulling back along
\alpha_{G}\colon G_{0}\sigma/G_{0}\to G\sigma/G
as before.
Thus we reduce to the case for
G_{0}
. As all constructions in the statement are compatible with direct products and the statement for a torus is trivial, we reduce to the case that
G
is simply connected.
In this case,
\chi=1
by Lemma
2.3.10
and
(-)\otimes\overline{{\mathcal{O}}_{\chi}}
is the identity. Thus we conclude.

For general
{\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}}
, note that the construction
(-)\otimes\overline{{\mathcal{O}}_{\chi}}
in
2
is
{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}}
-linear. Thus the isomorphism extends by linearity to all
{\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}}\in D\operatorname{Coh}(T\sigma/T)_{1}
.
∎

Then we can prove the main proposition for general
\chi
.

Proof of Proposition
2.3.1
.

We have already an isomorphism

J_{B^{\prime}|B,0}\in\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{eis}_{B},\operatorname{eis}_{B^{\prime}})_{1}
. We claim that

J_{B^{\prime}|B,0}\otimes\overline{{\mathcal{O}}_{\chi}}\in\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{eis}_{B},\operatorname{eis}_{B^{\prime}})_{\chi}

defined in Lemma
2.3.13
is an isomorphism and it is the desired intertwining operator.
Here the subscript
\chi
denotes the
\operatorname{Hom}
-module for the category
D\operatorname{Coh}(T\sigma/T)_{\chi}
.

Take a simply connected cover
G_{0}\to G
.
The statement that
J_{B^{\prime}|B,0}\otimes\overline{{\mathcal{O}}_{\chi}}
defines an isomorphism holds true by
\chi=1
case and the same descent arguments in the previous proofs. Moreover, by definition of the intertwining operator over the regular and semisimple locus, we have for any
{\mathcal{F}}\in D\operatorname{Coh}(T\sigma/T)
,

(J_{B^{\prime}|B,0}^{rs}\otimes\overline{{\mathcal{O}}_{\chi}})({\mathcal{F}})=J^{rs}_{B^{\prime}|B,0}({\mathcal{F}}_{\chi})

and thus
J_{B^{\prime}|B,0}\otimes\overline{{\mathcal{O}}_{\chi}}
is the extension of the intertwining operator in the regular semisimple locus. Finally, the statement that
J_{B^{\prime}|B,0}\otimes\overline{{\mathcal{O}}_{\chi}}
is a generator of the free module
\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{eis}_{B},\operatorname{eis}_{B^{\prime}})_{\chi}
follows from the
\chi=1
case and Lemma
2.3.13
.
∎

2.4
Extending to all

We will now extend the intertwining operator over the generic locus in
2.3.1
to the entire
{\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma}
and obtain the integral intertwining operator. More precisely, we will prove the following statement.

Proposition 2.4.1

(Integral intertwining operator for the principal block)
.

Let
\chi
be a character of
T^{\sigma}
which is invariant under the
W^{\sigma}
-action.
The homomorphism constructed in
2.3.1
,
J_{B^{\prime}|B}^{g}\colon\operatorname{Eis}_{B}^{g}\simeq\operatorname{Eis}_{B^{\prime}}^{g}
over
D\operatorname{Coh}({\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma}^{g})_{\chi}
can be uniquely extended to a homomorphism

j_{B^{\prime}|B}\colon\operatorname{Eis}_{B}\to\operatorname{Eis}_{B^{\prime}}
,
after the multiplication by
L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{{\mathfrak{n}}_{B^{\prime}}/{\mathfrak{n}}_{B}})^{-1}
. The result is denoted by
j_{B^{\prime}|B}
.
Moreover, the operator
j_{B^{\prime}|B}
generates the free
{\mathcal{O}}({\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma})
-module
\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{Eis}_{B},\operatorname{Eis}_{B^{\prime}})
.

We firstly consider the structure of the mild singularities of
{\mathbb{L}}_{B\sigma}
and
{\mathbb{L}}_{G\sigma}
.

We introduce the following notations.
By the explicit formula
2.1.3
of the L-function,
over the unique factorization ring
{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma})
,

L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{{\mathfrak{n}}\oplus\bar{{\mathfrak{n}}}})^{-1}
has no multiple factors.
Let
f
be an irreducible factor of
L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{{\mathfrak{n}}\oplus\bar{{\mathfrak{n}}}})^{-1}
, corresponding to the orbit
O
of roots. Let
{\mathbb{L}}_{B\sigma,f}
denote the non-vanishing locus of
L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{{\mathfrak{n}}\oplus\bar{{\mathfrak{n}}}})^{-1}/f
in
(B\sigma)^{rs}
. Similarly we define
(B\sigma)_{f}\subset B\sigma
,
{\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma,f}\subset{\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma}
and
(T\sigma)_{f}\subset T\sigma
. Let
{\mathbb{L}}_{B\sigma,f}^{\wedge}
denote the completion of
{\mathbb{L}}_{B\sigma,f}
along the zero locus
V(f)
of
f
. Similarly we define
{\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma,f}^{\wedge}
.

Lemma 2.4.2

(Mild singularities of
{\mathbb{L}}_{B\sigma}
)
.

Notations as above.

{\mathbb{L}}_{B\sigma,f}
is of the form

\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{O}((T\sigma)_{f})[u]/(fu))/T

over
(T\sigma)_{f}/T
if
f
divides
L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{{\mathfrak{n}}})^{-1}
and it is of the form
{\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma,f}\simeq(T\sigma)_{f}/T

if
f
divides
L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{\bar{{\mathfrak{n}}}})^{-1}
, where
T
acts on
T\sigma
by
\sigma
-conjugation and
T
acts on
u
by character
\alpha^{-1}
, for a chosen root
\alpha\in O
.

Moreover, the projection
{\mathbb{L}}_{B\sigma,f}\to{\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma.f}
is the annihilation of the coordinate
u

if
f
divides
L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{{\mathfrak{n}}})^{-1}
and it is an isomorphism if
f
divides
L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{\bar{{\mathfrak{n}}}})^{-1}
.

Proof.

Consider the pullback of
{\mathbb{L}}^{rs}_{B\sigma}
along the isomorphism
(T\sigma)^{rs}/T\to(B\sigma)^{rs}/B
. We have that

{\mathbb{L}}^{rs}_{B\sigma}\simeq\{(t,n)\in(T\sigma)^{rs}\times{\mathfrak{n}}\mid\mathrm{Ad}(t)(\sigma(n))=qn\}/T,

and similarly for
{\mathbb{L}}_{B\sigma,f}
.
By explicit description
2.1.3
of the
L
-function
L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{\mathfrak{n}})
, the irreducible factors of
L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{\mathfrak{n}})^{-1}
and
L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{\bar{{\mathfrak{n}}}})^{-1}
are disjoint.

If
f
does not divide
L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{\mathfrak{n}})^{-1}
,
\mathrm{Ad}_{\mathfrak{n}}(t)\circ\sigma-q
is invertible on
{\mathfrak{n}}
for
t\in{\mathbb{L}}_{B\sigma,f}
. Thus
