(t,n)\in{\mathbb{L}}_{B\sigma,f}
should satisfy
n=0
and
{\mathbb{L}}_{B\sigma,f}
is isomorphic to
{\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma,f}
.

If
f
divides
L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{\mathfrak{n}})^{-1}
, again by
2.1.3
,
f
divides
L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{{\mathfrak{n}}_{O}})^{-1}
for some orbit
O
and we have that

{\mathbb{L}}_{B\sigma,f}\simeq\{(t,n)\in(T\sigma)_{f}\times{\mathfrak{n}}_{O}\mid\mathrm{Ad}(t)(\sigma(n))=qn\}/T,

where
{\mathfrak{n}}_{O}=\bigoplus_{\alpha\in O}{\mathfrak{n}}_{\alpha}
, as the operator
\mathrm{Ad}_{\mathfrak{n}}(t)\circ\sigma-q
is invertible on
{\mathfrak{n}}/{\mathfrak{n}}_{O}
. Over
{\mathfrak{n}}_{O}
,
\sigma
is a cyclic permutation matrix (up to a sign) and
\mathrm{Ad}(t)
is an invertible diagonal matrix
\operatorname{diag}(\{\alpha(t)\}_{\alpha\in O})
under the basis in the pinning
\{X_{\alpha}\in{\mathfrak{n}}_{\alpha}\}
.

Fix an
\alpha\in O
. Then
n\in{\mathfrak{n}}_{O}
satisfying
\mathrm{Ad}(t)(\sigma(n))=qn
is uniquely determined by its coordinate
u_{\alpha}
in
{\mathfrak{n}}_{\alpha}
and the equation
\mathrm{Ad}(t)(\sigma(n))=qn

reduces to
L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{{\mathfrak{n}}_{O}})^{-1}u_{\alpha}=0
. More precisely, the projection

\{(t,n)\in(T\sigma)_{f}\times{\mathfrak{n}}_{O}\mid\mathrm{Ad}(t)(\sigma(n))=qn\}/T\to\{(t,n)\in(T\sigma)_{f}\times{\mathfrak{n}}_{\alpha}\mid L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{{\mathfrak{n}}_{O}})^{-1}u_{\alpha}\}/T

is an isomorphism.

Abbreviate
u_{\alpha}
by
u
. Then

{\mathbb{L}}_{B\sigma,f}\simeq\{(t,x)\in(T\sigma)_{f}\times k\mid fu=0\}/T\simeq\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{O}((T\sigma)_{f})[u]/(fu))/T.

As
T
acts on
{\mathfrak{n}}_{\alpha}
by the character
\alpha
,
T
acts on
u
by
\alpha^{-1}
.
∎

In the rank
1
case, the bad locus in
{\mathbb{L}}_{B\sigma}
decomposes into a disjoint union of irreducible components.

Lemma 2.4.3

(Rank
1
case)
.

If the Weyl group
W
satisfies
|W^{\sigma}|=2
, for any irreducible factor
f
of
L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{{\mathfrak{n}}\oplus\bar{{\mathfrak{n}}}})^{-1}
in
{\mathcal{O}}(T\sigma/T)
, its zero locus in
{\mathbb{L}}_{B\sigma}
(similarly for
B\sigma/B
) lies in
{\mathbb{L}}_{B\sigma}^{rs}
and these zero loci are mutually disjoint.

For
G
, we have a similar result:
Let
f
be as above. The zero locus of
fs(f)
in
{\mathbb{L}}_{G\sigma}
(similarly for
G\sigma/G
) is contained in the regular semisimple locus
{\mathbb{L}}_{G\sigma}^{rs}
and these zero loci are mutually disjoint.
Here, as the function
fs(f)
is
W^{\sigma}
-invariant, we may view it as a function on
G\sigma/G
and
{\mathbb{L}}_{G\sigma}
via the twisted Chevalley map.

Moreover, the morphism
p_{0}\colon B\sigma/B\to G\sigma/G
induces an isomorphism between
V(f)\subset B\sigma/B
and the zero locus of
fs(f)
in
G
,
V(fs(f))_{G}
for such
f
.

Proof.

In this case, there is a unique positive root
\alpha
of
W^{\sigma}
and the number of positive orbits is
1
(the orbit is denoted by
O
in this case) or
2
(the orbit is denoted by
O^{+}
,
O^{-}
in this case). Then by
2.1.3
, the
L
-function is

L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{{\mathfrak{n}}}|_{O})^{-1}=\begin{cases}1-q^{-|O|}e^{\alpha}(t)&\text{$\alpha$ is of type A}\\
(1+q^{-|O^{+}|}e^{\alpha}(t))(1-q^{-|O^{-}|}e^{\alpha}(t))&\text{$\alpha$ is of type BC}\end{cases}

Moreover,
{\mathbb{L}}_{B\sigma}^{rs}
is the nonzero locus of
L(0,\mathrm{Ad}_{{\mathfrak{n}}})^{-1}
, which is
1-e^{\alpha}(t)
or
(1-e^{\alpha}(t))(1-e^{2\alpha}(t))
according to the number of orbits.
In both cases, the zero loci of the factors of
L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{{\mathfrak{n}}\oplus\bar{{\mathfrak{n}}}})^{-1}
are disjoint and lie in
{\mathbb{L}}_{B\sigma}^{rs}
.

The statements about
G
follows similarly from the above description of the
L
-function. For the statement about
V(f)\to V(fs(f))_{G}
, note that
V(f)\subset(B\sigma)^{rs}/B
and
V(fs(f))_{G}\subset(G\sigma)^{rs}/G
. After restricting to
(B\sigma)^{rs}/B
,
p_{0}
is étale by Proposition
2.2.6
. Thus
p_{0}^{-1}(V(fs(f))_{G})=V(fs(f))\to V(fs(f))_{G}
is a 2-fold Galois covering. For
f
dividing
L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{{\mathfrak{n}}_{O}})^{-1}
,
s(f)
divides
L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{{\mathfrak{n}},s(O)})^{-1}
and we have
\alpha_{s(O)}=-\alpha_{O}
. Thus
V(f)
and
V(s(f))
are disjoint by descriptions of the
L
-function above. Thus
V(f)\to V(fs(f))_{G}
is an isomorphism.
∎

In particular, in the rank
1
case,
V(f)
is closed in
{\mathbb{L}}_{B\sigma}
.

Now we will pass to the completions along a point in
V(f)
or the entire
V(f)
in the rank
1
case.

Definition 2.4.4

(Completions)
.

Let
f
be a irreducible factor of
L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{{\mathfrak{n}}})^{-1}
in
{\mathcal{O}}(T\sigma/T)
.
Let
\pi_{G}\colon{\mathbb{L}}_{G\sigma}\to G\sigma/G
be the projection.

For general
G
, let
x\in V(f)
be a strongly regular semisimple element. We define
{\mathbb{L}}_{G\sigma,p_{0}(x)}^{\wedge}
as the completion of
{\mathbb{L}}_{G\sigma}
along
\pi_{G}^{-1}(p_{0}(x))
.

Moreover, in the rank
1
case, we define a global version:
{\mathbb{L}}_{G\sigma,f}^{\wedge}
is defined as the completion of
{\mathbb{L}}_{G\sigma}
along
\pi_{G}^{-1}(V(fs(f))
.

Here we consider the strongly regular semisimple points for the following reason: The completion of
p_{0}
at
x
,
(B\sigma/B)_{x}^{\wedge}\to(G\sigma/G)_{p_{0}(x)}^{\wedge}
is an isomorphism for strongly regular semisimple
x
, since
p_{0}
is étale at
x
and the isotropy subgroups at
x
and
p_{0}(x)
are isomorphic.

Lemma 2.4.5
.

Let
f
be a irreducible factor of
L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{{\mathfrak{n}}})^{-1}
in
{\mathcal{O}}(T\sigma/T)
.
The strongly regular semisimple points form a dense open set in
V(f)\subset B\sigma/B
.

Proof.

For any
w\in W^{\sigma}
, the action of
w
on
T_{\sigma}
defines an automorphism of
T_{\sigma}
. Thus for
w\neq 1
, there is a finite number of nontrivial characters
\chi_{1,w},\dots,\chi_{i_{w},w}
such that
x
is fixed by
w
if and only if
x
lies in the zero locus of
\chi_{j,w}-1
for each
j
.

The strongly regular semisimple locus is the complement of

\bigcup_{w\in W-\{1\}}\bigcap_{j}V(\chi_{j,w}-1).

Thus it is open. Moreover, by the explicit description of the
L
-function in Proposition
2.1.3
,
f
is a irreducible factor of
1\pm q^{-|O|}\alpha(t)
. Thus
f
does not divides any
\chi_{j,w}-1
and the intersection of
V(f)
and the strongly regular semisimple locus is dense.
∎

Lemma 2.4.6

(Mild singularities of
{\mathbb{L}}_{G\sigma}
)
.

Let
f
be a irreducible factor of
L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{{\mathfrak{n}}})^{-1}
in
{\mathcal{O}}(T\sigma/T)
and
x\in V(f)\subset B\sigma/B
be a strongly regular and semisimple point.
The pullback of
{\mathbb{L}}_{G\sigma,p(x)}^{\wedge}
along
p_{B}\colon{\mathbb{L}}_{B\sigma}\to{\mathbb{L}}_{G\sigma}
is of the form

\coprod_{w\in W^{\sigma}}{\mathbb{L}}_{B\sigma,w(x)}^{\wedge}

(note that there is a
W
-action on
(B\sigma)^{rs}/B\simeq(T\sigma)^{rs}/T
and
x\in(B\sigma)^{rs}/B
).

Moreover, under the representation of
{\mathbb{L}}_{B\sigma,f}
in Lemma
2.4.2
, the morphism
{\mathbb{L}}_{B\sigma,w(x)}^{\wedge}\to{\mathbb{L}}_{G\sigma,p_{0}(x)}^{\wedge}
can be identified with the identity on

\operatorname{Spf}(\mathcal{O}((T\sigma)_{w(x)}^{\wedge}[u]/(fu))/T

if
w(f)
divides
L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{\mathfrak{n}})^{-1}
, or it can be identified with

\operatorname{Spf}(\mathcal{O}(T\sigma)_{w(x)}^{\wedge})/T\to\operatorname{Spf}(\mathcal{O}(T\sigma)_{w(x)}^{\wedge}[u]/(w(f)u))/T

if
w(f)
divides
L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{\bar{{\mathfrak{n}}}})^{-1}
. Here
{\mathcal{O}}(T\sigma)_{x}^{\wedge}
is an abuse of notation for the completion of
{\mathcal{O}}(T\sigma)
along the inverse image of
x
in
T
.

Moreover, in the rank
1
case, the following global version holds.
The pullback of
{\mathbb{L}}_{G\sigma,f}^{\wedge}
along
p_{B}\colon{\mathbb{L}}_{B\sigma}\to{\mathbb{L}}_{G\sigma}
is of the form

\coprod_{w\in W^{\sigma}}{\mathbb{L}}_{B\sigma,w(f)}^{\wedge}.

Moreover, the morphism
{\mathbb{L}}_{B\sigma,w(f)}^{\wedge}\to{\mathbb{L}}_{G\sigma,f}^{\wedge}
can be identified with the identity on

\operatorname{Spf}(\mathcal{O}(T\sigma)_{w(f)}^{\wedge}[u]/(fu))/T

if
w(f)
divides
L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{\mathfrak{n}})^{-1}
, or it can be identified with

\operatorname{Spf}(\mathcal{O}(T\sigma)_{w(f)}^{\wedge}/T\to\operatorname{Spf}(\mathcal{O}(T\sigma)_{w(f)}^{\wedge}[u]/(w(f)u))/T

if
w(f)
divides
L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{\bar{{\mathfrak{n}}}})^{-1}
.

Proof.

We will consider the general case and the arguments in the rank
1
case hold in the same way.
Consider the following commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{L}}_{B\sigma}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{L}}_{B\sigma}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\sigma/B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{L}}_{G\sigma}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\sigma/G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{B}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{B}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{G}}

where
{\mathbb{L}}_{B\sigma}^{\prime}
is the pullback of the diagram
B\sigma/B\to G\sigma/G\leftarrow{\mathbb{L}}_{G\sigma}
. We calculate that

{\mathbb{L}}_{B\sigma}^{\prime}=\{(b\sigma,n)\in B\sigma\times{\mathcal{N}}\mid\mathrm{Ad}(b)(\sigma(n))=qn\}/B.

and thus
{\mathbb{L}}_{B\sigma}\to{\mathbb{L}}_{B\sigma}^{\prime}
is a closed immersion.

We claim that the pullback of
{\mathbb{L}}_{G\sigma,p_{0}(x)}^{\wedge}
along
p^{\prime}
is isomorphic to
|W^{\sigma}|
copies of itself and it is
\coprod_{w\in W^{\sigma}}{\mathbb{L}}_{B\sigma,\pi_{B}^{\prime-1}(w(x))}^{\prime\wedge}
explicitly. Indeed, for each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, since
p_{0}
is étale, the pullback of the
n
-th jet neighborhood of
p_{0}(x)
in
G\sigma/G
along
p_{0}
is the disjoint union of the
n
-th jet neighborhoods of
w(x)
in
B\sigma/B
for
w\in W^{\sigma}
. Pulling back along
\pi_{G}
, the pullback of the
n
-th jet neighborhood of
\pi_{G}^{-1}(p_{0}(x))
in
{\mathbb{L}}_{G\sigma}
along
p^{\prime}
is the disjoint union of the
n
-th jet neighborhoods of
\pi_{B}^{\prime-1}(w(x))
in
{\mathbb{L}}_{B\sigma}
. Passing to the limits and we prove the claim.

Then it suffices to determine each
{\mathbb{L}}_{B\sigma,\pi_{B}^{-1}(w(x))}^{\wedge}\to{\mathbb{L}}_{B\sigma,\pi_{B}^{\prime-1}(w(x))}^{\prime\wedge}
.
By
2.1.3
, denoting by
O
the orbit of roots corresponding to
f
, the formal neighborhood of
\pi_{B}^{\prime-1}(w(x))
lies in the substack

\{(b\sigma,n)\in B\sigma\times{\mathfrak{n}}_{w(O)}\mid\mathrm{Ad}(b)(\sigma(n))=qn\}/B

of
{\mathbb{L}}_{B\sigma}^{\prime}
and its inverse image in
{\mathbb{L}}_{B\sigma}
is in

\{(b\sigma,n)\in B\sigma\times{\mathfrak{n}}_{w(O)}\cap{\mathfrak{n}}\mid\mathrm{Ad}(b)(\sigma(n))=qn\}/B.

{\mathfrak{n}}\cap{\mathfrak{n}}_{w(O)}
is either
0
or
{\mathfrak{n}}_{w(O)}
, according to whether
w(f)
divides
L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{\mathfrak{n}})^{-1}
.
Thus the result follows from the calculations in
2.4.2
.
∎

The following corollary is a translation of the previous result in terms of
\operatorname{Eis}
.

Corollary 2.4.7
.

For an irreducible factor
f
of
L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{{\mathfrak{n}}\oplus\bar{{\mathfrak{n}}}})
and strongly regular semisimple
x\in V(f)
, consider
\operatorname{Eis}{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma,x}^{\wedge}}
as an

{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{L}}_{G\sigma,p_{0}(x)}^{\wedge}}
-module. Then
{\mathbb{L}}_{G\sigma,p_{0}(x)}^{\wedge}
can be identified with
\operatorname{Spf}(\mathcal{O}(T\sigma)_{x}^{\wedge}[u]/(fu))/T
and
\operatorname{Eis}{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma,x}^{\wedge}}
can be identified with:

•

The
\mathcal{O}(T\sigma)_{x}^{\wedge}[u]/(fu)
-module
\mathcal{O}(T\sigma)_{x}^{\wedge}[u]/(fu)
with its natural
T
-action (as in Lemma
2.4.2
) if
f
divides
L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{\mathfrak{n}})^{-1}
;

•

Or the
\mathcal{O}(T\sigma)_{x}^{\wedge}[u]/(fu)
-module
\mathcal{O}(T\sigma)_{x}^{\wedge}
with natural
T
-action and
u=0
if not.

Moreover, in the rank
1
case, the following global version holds.
Consider
\operatorname{Eis}{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma,f}^{\wedge}}
as an

{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{L}}_{G\sigma,f}^{\wedge}}
-module. Then
{\mathbb{L}}_{G\sigma,f}^{\wedge}
can be identified with
\operatorname{Spf}(\mathcal{O}(T\sigma)_{f}^{\wedge}[u]/(fu))/T
and
\operatorname{Eis}{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma,f}^{\wedge}}
can be identified with:

•

The
\mathcal{O}(T\sigma)_{f}^{\wedge}[u]/(fu)
-module
\mathcal{O}(T\sigma)_{f}^{\wedge}[u]/(fu)
with natural
T
-action if
f
divides
L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{\mathfrak{n}})^{-1}
;

•

Or the
\mathcal{O}(T\sigma)_{f}^{\wedge}[u]/(fu)
-module-module
\mathcal{O}(T\sigma)_{f}^{\wedge}
with natural
T
-action and
u=0
if not.

Next we construct an inclusion
\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{Eis}_{B},\operatorname{Eis}_{B^{\prime}})\to\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{eis}_{B},\operatorname{eis}_{B^{\prime}})
. For this purpose, we construct natural transformations
t_{l}\colon\pi_{G}^{*}\operatorname{eis}\to\operatorname{Eis}
and
t_{r}\colon\pi_{G*}\operatorname{Eis}\to\operatorname{eis}
as follows.

Consider the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{L}}_{B\sigma}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{L}}_{B\sigma}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\sigma/B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{L}}_{G\sigma}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\sigma/G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{B}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{B}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{G}}

(3)

where
{\mathbb{L}}_{B\sigma}^{\prime}
is the pullback of the diagram
B\sigma/B\to G\sigma/G\leftarrow{\mathbb{L}}_{G\sigma}
.

For a sheaf
\mathcal{F}
in
D\operatorname{Coh}({\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma})_{\chi}
, abusing the notation, denote also by
{\mathcal{F}}
its pullback via
B\sigma/B
.

We firstly construct
t_{l}
.
By proper base change, we have
\pi_{G}^{*}\operatorname{eis}_{B}{\mathcal{F}}\simeq p^{\prime}_{*}\pi_{B}^{\prime*}{\mathcal{F}}
. Moreover, we have
\operatorname{Eis}_{B}{\mathcal{F}}\simeq p_{*}\pi_{B}^{*}{\mathcal{F}}\simeq p^{\prime}_{*}i_{B*}^{\prime}i_{B}^{\prime*}\pi_{B}^{\prime*}{\mathcal{F}}
. Then we define

t_{l}\colon\pi_{G}^{*}\operatorname{eis}_{B}\simeq p^{\prime}_{*}\pi_{B}^{\prime*}\to p^{\prime}_{*}i_{B*}^{\prime}i_{B}^{\prime*}\pi_{B}^{\prime*}\simeq\operatorname{Eis}_{B}

by precomposing the adjunction
\mathbb{1}\to i_{B*}^{\prime}i_{B}^{\prime*}
by
\pi_{B}^{\prime*}
and then postcomposing
p^{\prime}_{*}
.
We remark that the adjunction of
t_{l}
,
\operatorname{eis}\to\pi_{*}\operatorname{Eis}_{B}
is given by

\operatorname{eis}_{B}=p_{0*}\xrightarrow{\text{adj}}p_{0*}\pi_{B*}^{\prime}\pi_{B}^{\prime*}\to p_{0*}\pi_{B*}^{\prime}i_{B*}^{\prime}i_{B}^{\prime*}\pi_{B}^{\prime*}\simeq p_{0*}\pi_{B*}\pi_{B}^{*}\simeq\pi_{G*}\operatorname{Eis}_{B}.

Then we construct
t_{r}
. In the commutative diagram above, we have
\pi_{G*}\operatorname{Eis}\simeq p_{0*}\pi_{B*}\pi_{B}^{*}
. As
\pi_{B*}\circ i_{B*}=\mathbb{1}
,
\operatorname{eis}_{B}\simeq p_{0*}\pi_{B*}i_{B*}
.
Moreover, there is an adjunction
\pi_{B}^{*}\to i_{B*}
of
1\xrightarrow{\sim}\pi_{B*}\circ i_{B*}
.
Then we define

t_{r}\colon\pi_{G*}\operatorname{Eis}_{B}\to\operatorname{eis}_{B}

by applying
p_{0*}\pi_{B*}
to the adjunction
\pi_{B}^{*}\to i_{B*}
.

We remark that
t_{r}\circ\operatorname{adj}(t_{l})
is the identity on
\operatorname{eis}_{B}
.

Definition 2.4.8

(Restriction)
.

For two sheaves
{\mathcal{F}}
and
{\mathcal{G}}
in
D\operatorname{Coh}({\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma})_{\chi}
, we define a map

\operatorname{res}\colon\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{Eis}_{B}{\mathcal{F}},\operatorname{Eis}_{B^{\prime}}{\mathcal{G}})\to\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{eis}_{B}{\mathcal{F}},\operatorname{eis}_{B^{\prime}}{\mathcal{G}})

by

\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{Eis}_{B}{\mathcal{F}},\operatorname{Eis}_{B^{\prime}}{\mathcal{G}})\xrightarrow{t_{l}({\mathcal{F}})^{*}}\operatorname{Hom}(\pi_{G}^{*}\operatorname{eis}_{B}{\mathcal{F}},\operatorname{Eis}_{B^{\prime}}{\mathcal{G}})\xrightarrow[\sim]{\text{adj}}\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{eis}_{B}{\mathcal{F}},\pi_{G*}\operatorname{Eis}_{B^{\prime}}{\mathcal{G}})\xrightarrow{t_{r}({\mathcal{G}})_{*}}\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{eis}_{B}{\mathcal{F}},\operatorname{eis}_{B^{\prime}}{\mathcal{G}}),

where we denote adj for the adjunction.

As the projection
{\mathbb{L}}_{G\sigma}^{g}\to(G\sigma)^{g}/G
is an isomorphism
(Lemma
2.2.2
), the following statement is immediate.

Lemma 2.4.9
.

After postcomposing
j^{g*}_{G}
, where
j^{g}_{G}
is the open immersion
{\mathbb{L}}_{G\sigma}^{g}\to{\mathbb{L}}_{G\sigma}
or
(G\sigma)^{g}/G\to G\sigma/G
,
t_{l}
and
t^{r}
become isomorphisms.

Note that
\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{Eis}_{B}{\mathcal{F}},\operatorname{Eis}_{B^{\prime}}{\mathcal{G}})
and
\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{eis}_{B}{\mathcal{F}},\operatorname{eis}_{B^{\prime}}{\mathcal{G}})
are
{\mathcal{O}}({\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma})={\mathcal{O}}(T\sigma/T)
-bimodules:

{\mathcal{O}}({\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma})={\mathcal{Z}}(D\operatorname{Coh}({\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma})_{1})
acts on
{\mathcal{F}}
and
{\mathcal{G}}
and then acts on
\operatorname{Eis}_{B}{\mathcal{F}}
and
\operatorname{Eis}_{B^{\prime}}{\mathcal{G}}
.
There is also a
{\mathcal{O}}(G\sigma/G)={\mathcal{O}}({\mathbb{L}}_{G\sigma})
-module structure on the
\operatorname{Hom}
’s, as the base space is
{\mathbb{L}}_{G\sigma}
or
G\sigma/G
and we have the following compatibility.

Lemma 2.4.10
.

Both the left and the right
{\mathcal{O}}(T\sigma/T)
-module structure of
\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{eis}_{B}{\mathcal{F}},\operatorname{eis}_{B^{\prime}}{\mathcal{G}})
and
\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{Eis}_{B}{\mathcal{F}},\operatorname{Eis}_{B^{\prime}}{\mathcal{G}})
are compatible with the
{\mathcal{O}}(G\sigma/G)
-structure, via the twisted Chevalley map
\chi^{*}\colon{\mathcal{O}}(G\sigma/G)\to{\mathcal{O}}(T\sigma/T)
.

Proof.

We consider the case for
\operatorname{eis}
, the case for
\operatorname{Eis}
is similar as the corresponding coarse moduli spaces are isomorphic.
For
f\in{\mathcal{O}}(G\sigma/G)
and
{\mathcal{F}}\in D\operatorname{Coh}(T\sigma/T)_{1}
, by Corollary
2.2.9
, the Chevalley map is equal to
{\mathcal{O}}(G\sigma/G)\xrightarrow{p_{0}^{*}}{\mathcal{O}}(B\sigma/B)\xleftarrow[\sim]{q_{0}^{*}}{\mathcal{O}}(T\sigma/T)
, where
q_{0}^{*}
is the inverse of
i_{0}^{*}\colon{\mathcal{O}}(B\sigma/B)\to{\mathcal{O}}(T\sigma/T)
induced by the inclusion
T\to B
.

By definition, the action of
f
on
\operatorname{eis}_{B}{\mathcal{F}}
is identified with the action obtained by applying the functor
p_{0*}
to the action of
p_{0}^{*}(f)
on
q_{0}^{*}{\mathcal{F}}
. Since
p_{0}^{*}(f)=q_{0}^{*}i_{0}^{*}p_{0}^{*}(f)=q_{0}^{*}\chi^{*}(f)
, the action of
\chi^{*}(f)\in{\mathcal{O}}(T\sigma/T)
on
\operatorname{eis}_{B}{\mathcal{F}}
and the action of
f\in{\mathcal{O}}(G\sigma/G)
coincide. Thus the
{\mathcal{O}}(G\sigma/G)
-structure is compatible with the
{\mathcal{O}}(T\sigma/T)
-module structure induced by
\operatorname{eis}_{B}
and similarly for
\operatorname{eis}_{B^{\prime}}
.
∎

Lemma 2.4.11
.

The map
\operatorname{res}
is an
{\mathcal{O}}({\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma})={\mathcal{O}}(T\sigma/T)
-bimodule homomorphism. Moreover, the construction
\operatorname{res}
preserves the monoidal structure in the following sense:
for an other Borel
B^{\prime\prime}
stable under
\sigma
and containing
T
, and an other sheaf
{\mathcal{H}}
over
T\sigma\sslash T
and homomorphisms
f\in\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{Eis}_{B}{\mathcal{F}},\operatorname{Eis}_{B^{\prime}}{\mathcal{G}})

and
g\in\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{Eis}_{B^{\prime}}{\mathcal{G}},\operatorname{Eis}_{B^{\prime\prime}}{\mathcal{H}})
, we have
\operatorname{res}(g\circ f)=\operatorname{res}(g)\circ\operatorname{res}(f)
.

Proof.

The
{\mathcal{O}}(T\sigma/T)
-linearity follows from the functoriality of
\operatorname{res}
. Then it suffices to prove the monoidality.
Replacing
{\mathcal{F}}
,
{\mathcal{G}}
and
{\mathcal{H}}
by free resolutions, it suffices to prove the case when
{\mathcal{F}}
,
{\mathcal{G}}
and
{\mathcal{H}}
are free
{\mathcal{O}}_{T\sigma/T}
-modules.
In this case,
\operatorname{eis}_{B}{\mathcal{F}}
and
\operatorname{eis}_{B^{\prime}}{\mathcal{G}}
are locally free
{\mathcal{O}}_{G\sigma/G}
-module by Lemma
2.3.9
. Hence, the homomorphism

\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{eis}_{B}{\mathcal{F}},\operatorname{eis}_{B^{\prime}}{\mathcal{G}})\to\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{eis}_{B}^{g}{\mathcal{F}},\operatorname{eis}_{B^{\prime}}^{g}{\mathcal{G}})

is injective as the generic locus is open and dense (Recall that
\operatorname{eis}_{B}^{g}{\mathcal{F}}
is an abuse of notation for
\operatorname{eis}_{B}^{g}j^{g*}_{T}{\mathcal{F}}\simeq j_{G}^{g*}\operatorname{eis}_{B}{\mathcal{F}}
).
Thus it suffices to prove that
\operatorname{res}
is monoidal after postcomposing the pullback
j^{g*}_{G}
.

In fact, by definition of
\operatorname{res}
and Lemma
2.4.9
, the composition

\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{Eis}_{B}{\mathcal{F}},\operatorname{Eis}_{B^{\prime}}{\mathcal{G}})\to\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{eis}_{B}{\mathcal{F}},\operatorname{eis}_{B^{\prime}}{\mathcal{G}})\to\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{eis}_{B}^{g}{\mathcal{F}},\operatorname{eis}_{B^{\prime}}^{g}{\mathcal{G}})=\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{Eis}_{B}^{g}{\mathcal{F}},\operatorname{Eis}_{B^{\prime}}^{g}{\mathcal{G}})

is just the restriction to the generic locus and thus it is monoidal. Thus
\operatorname{res}
is monoidal.
∎

In particular, the construction defines an
{\mathcal{O}}({\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma})={\mathcal{O}}(T\sigma/T)
-module homomorphism
\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{Eis}_{B},\operatorname{Eis}_{B^{\prime}})\to\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{eis}_{B},\operatorname{eis}_{B^{\prime}})
and a ring homomorphism
\operatorname{End}(\operatorname{Eis}_{B})\to\operatorname{End}(\operatorname{eis}_{B})
.

Next we prove that
\operatorname{res}
is injective.

Lemma 2.4.12
.

For
\chi\in X^{*}(T^{\sigma})^{W^{\sigma}}
and two locally free sheaves
{\mathcal{F}}
and
{\mathcal{G}}
in
\operatorname{Coh}({\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma})_{\chi}
,
the modules

R\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{Eis}_{B}{\mathcal{F}},\operatorname{Eis}_{B^{\prime}}{\mathcal{G}})\ \text{and}\ R\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{eis}_{B}{\mathcal{F}},\operatorname{eis}_{B^{\prime}}{\mathcal{G}})

are concentrated in degree
0
.
Moreover, the homomorphism

\operatorname{res}\colon\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{Eis}_{B}{\mathcal{F}},\operatorname{Eis}_{B^{\prime}}{\mathcal{G}})\to\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{eis}_{B}{\mathcal{F}},\operatorname{eis}_{B^{\prime}}{\mathcal{G}})

is injective,
and thus induces an inclusion
\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{Eis}_{B},\operatorname{Eis}_{B^{\prime}})\to\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{eis}_{B},\operatorname{eis}_{B^{\prime}})
for the functor version.

Proof.

We firstly prove the case that
\chi=1
.
By the unipotent Langlands correspondence proved by Zhu (
[
19
]
, Theorem 1.2), there is a fully faithful embedding (in the
\infty
-categorical sense)

D\mathsf{Rep}(G^{\vee}(F),I)\to D\operatorname{Coh}({\mathbb{L}}_{G\sigma})

for an unramified
p
-adic reductive group
G^{\vee}(F)
with Langlands dual
(G,\sigma)
(in the case that
\sigma=1
, it is already proved in
[
1
]
, Theorem 1.9).
It suffices to consider the case that
{\mathcal{F}}
and
{\mathcal{G}}
are free
{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}}
-modules only.
Under the correspondence,

{\mathcal{F}}
corresponds to a free module
M_{\mathcal{F}}
over
k[T^{\vee}/{T^{\vee}}^{0}]
and
\operatorname{Eis}_{B}{\mathcal{F}}
corresponds to
i_{B^{\vee}}^{G^{\vee}}M_{\mathcal{F}}
(
[
19
]
, Theorem 1.6(4), where
\operatorname{Eis}
is denoted by
\mathrm{CohSpr}
). Similarly for
{\mathcal{G}}
.

Thus we have

R\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{Eis}_{B}{\mathcal{F}},\operatorname{Eis}_{B^{\prime}}{\mathcal{G}})\simeq R\operatorname{Hom}(i_{B^{\vee}}^{G^{\vee}}M_{\mathcal{F}},i_{{B^{\prime}}^{\vee}}^{G^{\vee}}M_{\mathcal{G}}).

As
i_{B^{\vee}}^{G^{\vee}}M_{\mathcal{F}}
is projective in
\mathsf{Rep}(G^{\vee}(F))
, the latter
R\operatorname{Hom}
is concentrated in degree
0

and thus the previous one is. For the module
R\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{eis}_{B}{\mathcal{F}},\operatorname{eis}_{B^{\prime}}{\mathcal{G}})
,
it is concentrated in degree
0
as
\operatorname{eis}_{B}{\mathcal{F}}
is locally free by Lemma
2.3.9
.

Then we prove the argument about the inclusion.
For the same reason as in the proof of Lemma
2.4.11
, it suffices to prove that

\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{Eis}_{B}{\mathcal{F}},\operatorname{Eis}_{B^{\prime}}{\mathcal{G}})\to\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{eis}_{B}{\mathcal{F}},\operatorname{eis}_{B^{\prime}}{\mathcal{G}})\to\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{eis}^{g}_{B}{\mathcal{F}},\operatorname{eis}^{g}_{B^{\prime}}{\mathcal{G}})=\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{Eis}_{B}j^{g}_{*}j^{g*}{\mathcal{F}},\operatorname{Eis}_{B^{\prime}}j^{g}_{*}j^{g*}{\mathcal{G}})

is an inclusion,
where
j^{g}
is the inclusion of the generic locus into the entire space and the last arrow is from
j_{*}^{g}\circ\operatorname{Eis}^{g}\simeq\operatorname{Eis}\circ j^{g}_{*}
.
Under the local Langlands correspondence,
j^{g}_{*}j^{g*}{\mathcal{F}}
corresponds to the localization
M_{\mathcal{F}}[L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{{\mathfrak{n}}})L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{\bar{{\mathfrak{n}}}})]
as
j^{g}_{*}j^{g*}{\mathcal{F}}
is the colimit
{\mathcal{F}}\xrightarrow{L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{{\mathfrak{n}}\oplus\bar{{\mathfrak{n}}}})^{-1}}{\mathcal{F}}\to\cdots

and similarly for
M_{\mathcal{F}}[L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{{\mathfrak{n}}})L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{\bar{{\mathfrak{n}}}})]
.

Thus

\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{Eis}_{B}j^{g}_{*}j^{g*}{\mathcal{F}},\operatorname{Eis}_{B^{\prime}}j^{g}_{*}j^{g*}{\mathcal{G}})\simeq\operatorname{Hom}(i_{B^{\vee}}^{G^{\vee}}M_{\mathcal{F}}[f],i_{{B^{\prime}}^{\vee}}^{G^{\vee}}M_{\mathcal{G}}[f])

where
f=L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{\mathfrak{n}})L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{\bar{{\mathfrak{n}}}})
. Thus the homomorphism is injective as
M_{\mathcal{F}}\to M_{\mathcal{F}}[f]
and
M_{\mathcal{G}}\to M_{\mathcal{G}}[f]
is injective.

Finally, as
D\operatorname{Coh}({\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma})_{1}
is generated by
{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}}
,
we have
\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{Eis}_{B},\operatorname{Eis}_{B^{\prime}})\subset\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{Eis}_{B}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}},\operatorname{Eis}_{B^{\prime}}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}})
and
\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{eis}_{B},\operatorname{eis}_{B^{\prime}})\subset\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{eis}_{B}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}},\operatorname{eis}_{B^{\prime}}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}})
. Thus
\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{Eis}_{B},\operatorname{Eis}_{B^{\prime}})\to\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{eis}_{B},\operatorname{eis}_{B^{\prime}})
is an inclusion.

In the case for general
\chi
, take a simply connected cover
G_{0}\to G
. It induces a morphism
\alpha_{G}\colon{\mathbb{L}}_{G_{0}\sigma}\to\tilde{{\mathbb{L}}}_{G\sigma}/G_{0}\to{\mathbb{L}}_{G\sigma}
, which is a composition of a Galois covering and a gerbe. By descent, it suffices to prove the statements for
\alpha_{G}^{*}\operatorname{Eis}_{B}{\mathcal{F}}\simeq\operatorname{Eis}_{B_{0}}\alpha_{T}^{*}{\mathcal{F}}
and
\operatorname{Eis}_{B_{0}}\alpha_{T}^{*}{\mathcal{G}}
(where
\alpha_{T}\colon{\mathbb{L}}_{T_{0}\sigma}\to{\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma}
. Thus we reduce to the case for
G_{0}
.
Again, the constructions in the statement are compatible with direct products, the statement for a torus is trivial and the statement for a semisimple and simply connected group follows from the
\chi=1
case. Thus we conclude.
∎

Remark 2.4.13
.

There may be a simpler proof of the injective argument. By definition of the restriction homomorphism
2.4.8
,
\operatorname{res}
is the composition

\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{Eis}_{B}{\mathcal{F}},\operatorname{Eis}_{B^{\prime}}{\mathcal{G}})\xrightarrow{t_{l}({\mathcal{F}})^{*}}\operatorname{Hom}(\pi_{G}^{*}\operatorname{eis}_{B}{\mathcal{F}},\operatorname{Eis}_{B^{\prime}}{\mathcal{G}})\xrightarrow[\sim]{\text{adj}}\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{eis}_{B}{\mathcal{F}},\pi_{G*}\operatorname{Eis}_{B^{\prime}}{\mathcal{G}})\xrightarrow{t_{r}({\mathcal{G}})_{*}}\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{eis}_{B}{\mathcal{F}},\operatorname{eis}_{B^{\prime}}{\mathcal{G}}).

Thus it suffices to prove that
t_{l}({\mathcal{F}})^{*}
is injective and
t_{r}({\mathcal{G}})_{*}
is an isomorphism. Indeed, we can prove that
t_{r}({\mathcal{G}})_{*}
is an isomorphism and
t_{l}({\mathcal{F}})^{*}
is injective, supposing that
t_{l}({\mathcal{F}})
is a surjection of honest sheaves if
{\mathcal{F}}
is locally free. However, we do not know how to prove this statement in general.

The following lemma about the
\operatorname{Hom}
-modules follows from the same argument as in the proof of Lemma
2.3.13
.

Lemma 2.4.14
.

For
\chi\in X^{*}(T^{\sigma})^{W^{\sigma}}
and two locally free sheaves

{\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}}\in\operatorname{Coh}({\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma})_{1}
, the isomorphism in Lemma
2.3.13

\otimes\overline{{\mathcal{O}}_{\chi}}\colon\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{eis}_{B}{\mathcal{F}},\operatorname{eis}_{B}{\mathcal{G}})\to\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{eis}_{B}{\mathcal{F}}_{\chi},\operatorname{eis}_{B}{\mathcal{G}}_{\chi})

restricts to an isomorphism (under the inclusion in Lemma
2.4.12
)

\otimes\overline{{\mathcal{O}}_{\chi}}\colon\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{Eis}_{B}{\mathcal{F}},\operatorname{Eis}_{B}{\mathcal{G}})\to\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{Eis}_{B}{\mathcal{F}}_{\chi},\operatorname{Eis}_{B}{\mathcal{G}}_{\chi}).

Moreover, this isomorphism extends to all
{\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}}\in D\operatorname{Coh}({\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma})_{1}
.

We turn to the final proof of Proposition
2.4.1
.

Proof.

By lemma
2.4.12
above, we have that

\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{Eis}_{B},\operatorname{Eis}_{B^{\prime}})\subset\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{eis}_{B},\operatorname{eis}_{B^{\prime}})\simeq{\mathcal{O}}({\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma})

where the isomorphism can be inferred from the proof of
2.3.1
, making
\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{Eis}_{B},\operatorname{Eis}_{B^{\prime}})
an ideal of
{\mathcal{O}}({\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma})
. As in the proof of
2.2.2
, the operator
J^{g}_{B^{\prime}|B}
extends to an isomorphism
\operatorname{eis}_{B}\simeq\operatorname{eis}_{B^{\prime}}
and corresponds to
1\in{\mathcal{O}}({\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma})
, it suffices to find an element
m\in{\mathcal{O}}({\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma})
such that
mJ^{g}_{B^{\prime}|B}
induces a homomorphism
\operatorname{Eis}_{B}\to\operatorname{Eis}_{B^{\prime}}
and such that any other such multiplier is divisible by
m
.

In the beginning, we consider the case for
\chi=1
.
We firstly prove that
m
is divisible by
L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{{\mathfrak{n}}_{B^{\prime}}/{\mathfrak{n}}_{B}})^{-1}
. For any irreducible factor
f
of
L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{{\mathfrak{n}}_{B^{\prime}}})^{-1}
that does not divide
L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{{\mathfrak{n}}_{B}})^{-1}
and strongly regular semisimple
x\in V(f)
,
consider

\operatorname{Eis}_{B}{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma,x}^{\wedge}}
and
\operatorname{Eis}_{B^{\prime}}{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma,x}^{\wedge}}
as

{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{L}}_{G\sigma,p_{0}(x)}^{\wedge}}
-modules. By Corollary
2.4.7
,
{\mathbb{L}}_{G\sigma,p_{0}(x)}^{\wedge}
can be identified with
\operatorname{Spf}(\mathcal{O}(T\sigma)_{x}^{\wedge}[u]/(fu))/T
,
\operatorname{Eis}_{B^{\prime}}{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma,x}^{\wedge}}
can be identified with the
\mathcal{O}(T\sigma)_{x}^{\wedge}[u]/(fu)
-module
\mathcal{O}(T\sigma)_{x}^{\wedge}[u]/(fu)
with natural
T
-action and
\operatorname{Eis}_{B}{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma,x}^{\wedge}}
can be identified with the
\mathcal{O}(T\sigma)_{x}^{\wedge}[u]/(fu)
-module
\mathcal{O}(T\sigma)_{x}^{\wedge}
with natural
T
-action and
u=0
.

Thus
mJ^{g}_{B^{\prime}|B}
defines an
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{L}}_{G\sigma,p_{0}(x)}^{\wedge}}
-module homomorphism
\operatorname{Eis}_{B}{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma,x}^{\wedge}}\to\operatorname{Eis}_{B^{\prime}}{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma,x}^{\wedge}}
only if
m
is divisible by
f
in the formal neighborhood of
x
, as
u=0
in
\mathcal{O}(T\sigma)_{x}^{\wedge}
is sent to
mu=0
in
\mathcal{O}(T\sigma)_{x}^{\wedge}[u]/(fu))
. Varying
x
in the open and dense subset of strongly regular semisimple elements in
V(f)
, we see that
m=0
on
V(f)
and thus
m
is divisible by
f
as
f
is irreducible and
{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma})
is factorial.
Multiplying all those
f
, we have that
m
is divisible by
L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{{\mathfrak{n}}_{B^{\prime}}/{\mathfrak{n}}_{B}})^{-1}
as
L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{{\mathfrak{n}}_{B^{\prime}}/{\mathfrak{n}}_{B}})^{-1}
has no multiple factors by
2.1.3
.

It suffices to show that
L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{{\mathfrak{n}}_{B^{\prime}}/{\mathfrak{n}}_{B}})^{-1}J^{g}_{B^{\prime}|B}
defines a transformation
\operatorname{Eis}_{B}\to\operatorname{Eis}_{B^{\prime}}
.

Let
B=B_{1},B_{2},\dots,B_{n}=B^{\prime}
be a chain of Borel subgroups in
G
fixed by
\sigma
such that for any
i
, there exists a simple reflection
s_{i}\in W^{\sigma}
such that
B_{i+1}=s_{i}B_{i}s_{i}
and that
n
is the minimal length of the chain (such chain exists as Borel subgroups of
G
fixed by
\sigma
correspond to Weyl chamber of the root system
\{X^{*}(T_{\sigma}),\{\alpha_{O}\}\}
). For
i
, denote by
P_{i}
the minimal parabolic subgroup containing
B_{i}
and
B_{i+1}
and denote by
M_{i}
its Levi subgroup. By
[
10
]
, Corollary 2.11 (for the case
\sigma=1
, and the general case is similar), for
j=i
or
j=i+1
we have

\operatorname{Eis}_{B_{j}}^{G}=\operatorname{Eis}_{P_{i}}^{G}\circ\operatorname{Eis}_{B_{j}\cap M_{i}}^{M_{i}}

Moreover, we have that the
L
function is multiplicative:

L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{{\mathfrak{n}}_{B^{\prime}}/{\mathfrak{n}}_{B}})=\prod_{i}L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{{\mathfrak{n}}_{B_{i+1}}/{\mathfrak{n}}_{B_{i}}})=\prod_{i}L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{{\mathfrak{n}}_{B_{i+1}\cap M_{i}}/{\mathfrak{n}}_{B_{i}\cap M_{i}}})

It suffices to prove that

L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{{\mathfrak{n}}_{B_{i+1}\cap M_{i}}/{\mathfrak{n}}_{B_{i}\cap M_{i}}})^{-1}J^{g}_{B_{i+1}\cap M_{i}|B_{i}\cap M_{i}}\colon\operatorname{Eis}_{B_{i}\cap M_{i}}\to\operatorname{Eis}_{B_{i+1}\cap M_{i}}

is well-defined. Note that
M_{i}
satisfies the condition in Lemma
2.4.3
. Thus for any irreducible factor
f
of
L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{{\mathfrak{n}}_{M_{i}}\oplus\bar{{\mathfrak{n}}}_{M_{i}}})^{-1}
,
V(f)\subset{\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma,f}
is exactly the vanishing locus of
f
in
{\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma}
. Moreover, different
V(f)
’s are disjoint and the non-generic locus (relative to
M_{i}
) of
{\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma}
is the union of those
V(f)
’s.
Thus by Beauville–Laszlo gluing, it suffices to prove that
L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{{\mathfrak{n}}_{B_{i+1}\cap M_{i}}/{\mathfrak{n}}_{B_{i}\cap M_{i}}})^{-1}J^{g}_{B_{i+1}\cap M_{i}|B_{i}\cap M_{i}}
is well-defined over the formal neighborhood of
V(f)
for all such
f
.
That is, for
V(f)\subset{\mathbb{L}}_{T}-{\mathbb{L}}_{T}^{g}
,
L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{{\mathfrak{n}}_{B_{i+1}\cap M_{i}}/{\mathfrak{n}}_{B_{i}\cap M_{i}}})^{-1}J^{g}_{B_{i+1}\cap M_{i}|B_{i}\cap M_{i}}({\mathcal{O}}(T\sigma)^{\wedge}_{f})
is well-defined.

By Corollary
2.4.7
, the homomorphism
L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{{\mathfrak{n}}_{B_{i+1}\cap M_{i}}/{\mathfrak{n}}_{B_{i}\cap M_{i}}})^{-1}J^{g}_{B_{i+1}\cap M_{i}|B_{i}\cap M_{i}}({\mathcal{O}}(T\sigma)^{\wedge}_{f})

is of the form

\text{multiplying by}\ L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{{\mathfrak{n}}_{B_{i+1}\cap M_{i}}/{\mathfrak{n}}_{B_{i}\cap M_{i}}})^{-1}\colon N\to N^{\prime}

where the
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{L}}_{G\sigma,f}^{\wedge}}
-modules
N
and
N^{\prime}
are of the following forms:
N
can be identified with the module

\mathcal{O}(T\sigma)_{f}^{\wedge}[u]/(fu)
or
\mathcal{O}(T\sigma)_{f}^{\wedge}
with natural
T
-action,
according to whether
f
divides
L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{{\mathfrak{n}}_{B_{i}\cap M_{i}}})^{-1}
and similarly for
N^{\prime}
, according to whether
f
divides
L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{{\mathfrak{n}}_{B_{i+1}\cap M_{i}}})^{-1}
. In the case that
f
divides
L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{{\mathfrak{n}}_{B_{i+1}\cap M_{i}}})^{-1}
but does not divide
L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{{\mathfrak{n}}_{B_{i}\cap M_{i}}})^{-1}
, the module homomorphism
L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{{\mathfrak{n}}_{B_{i+1}\cap M_{i}}/{\mathfrak{n}}_{B_{i}\cap M_{i}}})^{-1}J^{g}_{B_{i+1}\cap M_{i}|B_{i}\cap M_{i}}({\mathcal{O}}(T\sigma)^{\wedge}_{f})
is well-defined, being identified as a multiple of
\cdot f\colon\mathcal{O}(T\sigma)_{f}^{\wedge}\to\mathcal{O}(T\sigma)_{f}^{\wedge}[u]/(fu)
. For other possibilities of
f
, multiplication by
1
suffices to obtain a well-defined homomorphism. Thus multiplying by
L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{{\mathfrak{n}}_{B_{i+1}\cap M_{i}}/{\mathfrak{n}}_{B_{i}\cap M_{i}}})^{-1}
is always a well-defined homomorphism.

Finally, we prove the case for general
\chi
. We have already an intertwining operator
j_{B^{\prime}|B}\in\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{Eis}_{B},\operatorname{Eis}_{B^{\prime}})_{1}
. We claim that

j_{B^{\prime}|B}\otimes\overline{{\mathcal{O}}_{\chi}}\in\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{Eis}_{B},\operatorname{Eis}_{B^{\prime}})_{\chi}

is the desired intertwining operator. Here the subscripts means the
\operatorname{Hom}
-module over the corresponding subcategory of
D\operatorname{Coh}({\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma})
.

Recall that in the proof of Proposition
2.3.1
(in the end of section 2.3), the intertwining operator in
\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{Eis}^{g}_{B},\operatorname{Eis}^{g}_{B^{\prime}})_{\chi}
is defined as
J^{g}_{B^{\prime}|B}\otimes\overline{{\mathcal{O}}_{\chi}}
, where
J^{g}_{B^{\prime}|B}\in\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{Eis}^{g}_{B},\operatorname{Eis}^{g}_{B^{\prime}})_{1}
. Thus we have

j_{B^{\prime}|B}\otimes\overline{{\mathcal{O}}_{\chi}}=L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{{\mathfrak{n}}_{B^{\prime}}/{\mathfrak{n}}_{B}})^{-1}(J_{B^{\prime}|B}^{g}\otimes\overline{{\mathcal{O}}_{\chi}})

and we prove the statement about the multiplier. The statement about the generator follows from the
\chi=1
case and Lemma
2.4.14
.
∎

Thus we proved Theorem
1.2.2
for the in the unipotent case.

2.5
Adjunction

We discuss the left adjoint functor of
\operatorname{Eis}=p_{*}q^{*}
and study its properties in this section. We use the same notations as in the previous sections.

We will denote by
\operatorname{CT}_{B}
the putative left adjoint of
\operatorname{Eis}_{B}
and consider the composition
\operatorname{CT}_{B}\circ\operatorname{Eis}_{B}
. By definition,

\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{CT}_{B}\circ\operatorname{Eis}_{B}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}},{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}})=\operatorname{End}(\operatorname{Eis}_{B}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}})\subset\operatorname{End}(\operatorname{eis}_{B}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}})

where the inclusion is induced by
\operatorname{res}
, as in Lemma
2.4.12
.
This construction motivates us to consider the endomorphism ring
\operatorname{End}(\operatorname{eis}_{B}{\mathcal{O}}_{T})
.

In the next lemmas we sometimes consider simply connected reductive groups, by which we mean products of a simply connected semisimple group and a torus. Moreover, when we consider a simply connected cover
G_{0}\to G
, we always require that the automorphism
\sigma
of
G
can be lifted to an automorphism
\sigma
of
G_{0}
.

Lemma 2.5.1
.

Fix a covering
G_{0}\to G
as in the diagram
1
. We have morphisms
p_{T}\colon T_{\sigma}=T\sigma\sslash T\to T\sigma\sslash^{\prime}N_{G}(T\sigma)
and

\pi_{T}\colon G\sigma/G_{0}\to T\sigma\sslash^{\prime}N_{G}(T\sigma)
.
The endomorphism ring
\operatorname{End}(\operatorname{eis}_{B}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}})
is isomorphic to the endomorphism ring

\operatorname{End}_{T\sigma\sslash^{\prime}N_{G}(T\sigma)}(p_{T*}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}}).

Moreover, consider the morphism
\eta\colon T\sigma\sslash^{\prime}N_{G}(T\sigma)\to T_{\sigma}\sslash W^{\sigma}
from an algebraic stack to its coarse moduli space.
Then the homomorphism

\eta_{*}\colon\operatorname{End}_{T\sigma\sslash^{\prime}N_{G}(T\sigma)}(p_{T*}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}})\to\operatorname{End}_{T_{\sigma}\sslash W^{\sigma}}(\eta_{*}p_{T*}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}})=\operatorname{End}_{{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma})^{W^{\sigma}}}({\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma}))

is injective and when
G
is simply connected, it is an isomorphism.

Proof.

We firstly construct an isomorphism
\operatorname{End}(\operatorname{eis}_{B}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}})\simeq\operatorname{End}_{T\sigma\sslash^{\prime}N_{G}(T\sigma)}(p_{T*}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}})
.
Recall that we have a gerbe
\beta\colon G\sigma/G_{0}\to G\sigma/G
and we have
\operatorname{End}(\operatorname{eis}_{B}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}})\simeq\operatorname{End}(\beta^{*}\operatorname{eis}_{B}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}})
.
By Lemma
2.3.9
,
\beta^{*}\operatorname{eis}_{B}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}}
is isomorphic to
\pi_{T}^{*}p_{T*}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}}
.
We claim that
\pi_{T}^{*}
induces an isomorphism
\operatorname{End}(p_{T*}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}})\to\operatorname{End}(\beta^{*}\operatorname{eis}_{B}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}})
.
It suffices to prove that the unit map

p_{T*}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}}\xrightarrow{\sim}\pi_{T*}\pi_{T}^{*}p_{T*}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}}

is an isomorphism.

When
G=G_{0}
is simply connected,
T\sigma\sslash^{\prime}N_{G}(T\sigma)\simeq T\sigma\sslash N_{G}(T\sigma)
is a scheme. Moreover, by Chevalley isomorphism
2.1.5
,
\pi_{T}
is a morphism from an algebraic stack to its coarse moduli space. Thus in this case the unit map
\mathbb{1}\to\pi_{T*}\pi_{T}^{*}
is an isomorphism and the lemma holds.

In general, as in the diagram in Proposition
2.3.6
,
\pi_{T}
is decomposed into

G\sigma/G_{0}\xrightarrow{\pi_{T}^{\prime}}(T_{0}\sigma\sslash N_{G_{0}}(T_{0}\sigma))/\operatorname{Gal}(G_{0}/G)\xrightarrow{\pi_{T}^{\prime\prime}}T\sigma\sslash^{\prime}N_{G}(T\sigma).

By pulling back via the Galois covering
T_{0}\sigma\sslash N_{G_{0}}(T_{0}\sigma)\to T_{0}\sigma\sslash N_{G_{0}}(T_{0}\sigma)/\operatorname{Gal}(G_{0}/G)
and the result in the
G=G_{0}
case, we have the unit map
\mathbb{1}\to\pi^{\prime}_{T*}\pi^{\prime*}_{T}
is an isomorphism. Moreover, as
\pi_{T}^{\prime\prime}
is a
\ker(\operatorname{Gal}(G_{0}/G)\to T_{0,\sigma})
-gerbe, the unit map
\mathbb{1}\to\pi^{\prime\prime}_{T*}\pi^{\prime\prime*}_{T}
is an isomorphism.
Thus the unit map
\mathbb{1}\to\pi_{T*}\pi^{*}_{T}
is an isomorphism.

Then we prove that
\eta_{*}
is injective. Over the open subset
T^{s.rs}_{\sigma}
consisting of strongly regular semisimple elements of
T_{\sigma}
,

W^{\sigma}
acts freely and thus
T^{s.rs}_{\sigma}\sslash W^{\sigma}=T^{s.rs}_{\sigma}/W^{\sigma}
. Moreover, the group
\ker(T_{0,\sigma}\to T_{\sigma})\times W^{\sigma}

acts freely on the inverse image of
T^{s,rs}_{\sigma}
in
T_{0,\sigma}
, denoted
T_{0,\sigma}^{s.rs}
. Thus

(T\sigma)^{s.rs}\sslash^{\prime}N_{G}(T\sigma)\simeq T_{0,\sigma}^{s.rs}/(\ker(T_{0,\sigma}\to T_{\sigma})\times W^{\sigma})\simeq T_{\sigma}^{s.rs}/W^{\sigma}\simeq T_{\sigma}^{s.rs}\sslash W^{\sigma}

and
\eta
is an isomorphism over
T_{\sigma}^{s.rs}
. As
T^{s.rs}_{\sigma}
is open and dense in
T_{\sigma}
and
p_{T*}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}}
is torsion-free, denoting by
j^{s.rs}\colon T^{s.rs}_{\sigma}\subset T_{\sigma}
the open immersion,

j^{s.rs*}\eta_{*}\colon\operatorname{End}_{T\sigma\sslash^{\prime}N_{G}(T\sigma)}(p_{T*}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}})\to\operatorname{End}_{T_{\sigma}^{s.rs}\sslash W^{\sigma}}(j^{s.rs*}\eta_{*}p_{T*}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}})

is injective. Thus
\eta_{*}
is injective. Moreover, when
G=G_{0}
is simply connected,
T\sigma\sslash^{\prime}N_{G}(T\sigma)=T_{\sigma}\sslash W^{\sigma}
and thus
\eta_{*}
is an isomorphism.
∎

We will describe the endomorphism ring of
{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma})
as an
{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma})^{W^{\sigma}}
-module
via the following notion of (multiplicative) Demazure operators.

Definition 2.5.2

(Demazure operators)
.

For a simple reflection
s_{\alpha}
in
W^{\sigma}
, define the Demazure operator
\delta_{s_{\alpha}}
as

\delta_{s_{\alpha}}\colon{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma})\to{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma}),\quad f\mapsto\frac{f-s_{\alpha}(f)}{1-e^{\tilde{\alpha}}},

where
\tilde{\alpha}
is defined in the following process:

•

Suppose that
G
is simply connected. Then
\tilde{\alpha}
is defined to be

2\alpha
or
\alpha
according to whether
\alpha^{\vee}\in X_{*}(T_{\sigma})
is divisible by
2
or not.

•

For general
G
, take a simply connected cover
G_{0}\to G
. Then the set of roots
R_{G_{0}}
of
G_{0}
and
R_{G}
of
G
can be naturally identified and similarly for the
\sigma
-orbits
R_{G_{0},\sigma}
and
R_{G,\sigma}
in the root system. Denote by
\pi\colon R_{G_{0},\sigma}\operatorname{\xrightarrow{\sim}}R_{G,\sigma}
the identification. Then
\tilde{\alpha}
is defined to be
\pi(\widetilde{\pi^{-1}(\alpha)})
. We remark that this construction is independent of
G_{0}
,
as it depends only on the semisimple group part of
G_{0}
.

For general
w\in W^{\sigma}
, let
w=s_{1}\dots s_{n}
be a reduced expression of
w
, define
\delta_{w}:=\delta_{s_{1}}\dots\delta_{s_{n}}
(by the same proof as for the additive case in Théorème 1 in
[
4
]
, it is independent of the choice of
s_{1}\dots s_{n}
).

The definition is a multiplicative analog of the results in
[
4
]
, where
T
is replaced by its Lie algebra
\mathfrak{t}
and

\delta_{s}
is defined by
D_{s}:=\frac{1-s}{\alpha_{s}}
and the operators is defined as
D_{w}:=D_{s_{1}}\dots D_{s_{n}}
.

By definition,
\delta_{w}
is
{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma})^{W^{\sigma}}
-linear and can be viewed as an element in
\operatorname{End}_{{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma})^{W_{\sigma}}}({\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma}))
. We will see that it lies in

\operatorname{End}(\operatorname{eis}_{B}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}})
.

Recall that for a simply connected cover
G\to G_{0}
, in the proof of Proposition
2.3.6
, we have the following cartesian diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{0,\sigma}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{\sigma}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{0}\sigma\sslash N_{G_{0}}(T_{0}\sigma)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\sigma\sslash^{\prime}N_{G}(T\sigma),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{T_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

where the horizontal arrows are Galois coverings with Galois group
\Gamma^{\prime}=\ker(T_{0,\sigma}\to T_{\sigma})
. Thus we have
p_{T_{0}*}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{0,\sigma}}\simeq\pi^{*}p_{T*}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}}
and we have a
\Gamma^{\prime}
-equivariant isomorphism

\operatorname{End}_{T\sigma\sslash^{\prime}N_{G}(T\sigma)}(p_{T*}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}})\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma})}{\mathcal{O}}(T_{0,\sigma})\simeq\operatorname{End}_{T_{0}\sigma\sslash N_{G_{0}}(T_{0}\sigma)}(p_{T_{0}*}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{0,\sigma}})=\operatorname{End}_{{\mathcal{O}}(T_{0,\sigma})^{W^{\sigma}}}({\mathcal{O}}(T_{0,\sigma})).

We will show the compatibility of Demazure operators under coverings.

Lemma 2.5.3
.

Under the inclusion in Lemma
2.5.1
,

\operatorname{End}(\operatorname{eis}_{B}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}})\subset\operatorname{End}_{{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma})^{W^{\sigma}}}({\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma})),

\delta_{w}\in\operatorname{End}_{{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma})^{W^{\sigma}}}({\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma}))
lies in

\operatorname{End}(\operatorname{eis}_{B}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}})
.

Moreover, for a simply connected cover
G_{0}\to G
,
denote by
\delta_{w,T_{0}}
the operator for
T_{0,\sigma}

and
\delta_{w,T}
the operator for
T_{\sigma}
.
we have the following compatibility between
\delta_{w,T_{0}}
and
\delta_{w,T}
:
Under the identity

\operatorname{End}_{T\sigma\sslash^{\prime}N_{G}(T\sigma)}(p_{T*}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}})\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma})}{\mathcal{O}}(T_{0,\sigma})\simeq\operatorname{End}_{T_{0}\sigma\sslash N_{G_{0}}(T_{0}\sigma)}(p_{T_{0}*}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{0,\sigma}})=\operatorname{End}_{{\mathcal{O}}(T_{0,\sigma})^{W^{\sigma}}}({\mathcal{O}}(T_{0,\sigma}))

(4)

above,
\delta_{w,T}\otimes 1
in the left hand side is equal to

\delta_{w,T_{0}}
in the right hand side.

Proof.

We claim that
\delta_{w,T_{0}}\in\operatorname{End}_{{\mathcal{O}}(T_{0,\sigma})^{W^{\sigma}}}({\mathcal{O}}(T_{0,\sigma}))
lies in
\operatorname{End}_{T\sigma\sslash^{\prime}N_{G}(T\sigma)}(p_{T*}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}})\otimes 1
under the identity
4
above. Any element
\gamma
in
\ker(T_{0,\sigma}\to T_{\sigma})

is
W^{\sigma}
-invariant and satisfies
\alpha(\gamma)=1
for any root
\alpha
of
T_{\sigma}
. Thus the following diagram commutes for any
\gamma\in\ker(T_{0,\sigma}\to T_{\sigma})
and
w\in W^{\sigma}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{O}}(T_{0,\sigma})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{O}}(T_{0,\sigma})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{O}}(T_{0,\sigma})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{O}}(T_{0,\sigma}),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{w}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{\gamma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{\gamma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{w}}

where
t_{\gamma}
denotes the translation by
\gamma
.
Hence
\delta_{w}
for
T_{0,\sigma}
is invariant under the
\ker(T_{0,\sigma}\to T_{0})
-action, proving the claim by Galois descent.

It suffices to prove that
\delta_{w,T_{0}}
equals
\delta_{w,T}
, viewed as elements in
\operatorname{End}_{{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma})^{W^{\sigma}}}({\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma}))

under the inclusion in Lemma
2.5.1
.
Consider the strongly regular semisimple locus
j^{s.rs}\colon T^{s.rs}_{\sigma}\subset T_{\sigma}
,
which is open and dense. It suffices to prove that they are equal in
\operatorname{End}_{T^{s.rs}_{\sigma}/W^{\sigma}}(j^{s.rs*}p_{T*}{\mathcal{O}}_{T^{s.rs}_{\sigma}})
.

Recall that in the proof of Lemma
2.5.1
, we have

(T\sigma)^{s.rs}\sslash^{\prime}N_{G}(T\sigma)\simeq T_{\sigma}^{s.rs}\sslash W^{\sigma}
.
Thus the identity
4
becomes

\operatorname{End}_{T^{s.rs}_{\sigma}/W^{\sigma}}(j^{s.rs*}p_{T*}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}})\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma})}{\mathcal{O}}(T_{0,\sigma})\simeq\operatorname{End}_{T_{0,\sigma}^{s.rs}/W^{\sigma}}(j^{s.rs*}p_{T_{0}*}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{0,\sigma}})

after localization on
T^{s.rs}_{\sigma}

(
T_{0,\sigma}^{s.rs}
is defined to be the inverse image of
T^{s.rs}_{\sigma}
in
T_{0,\sigma}
). Via this identity,
\delta_{w,T_{0}}
and
\delta_{w,T}
are equal in

\operatorname{End}_{T_{0,\sigma}^{s.rs}/W^{\sigma}}(j^{s.rs*}p_{T_{0}*}{\mathcal{O}}(T_{0,\sigma}))
,
as the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{O}}(T_{0,\sigma})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{O}}(T_{0,\sigma}),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{w,T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{w,T_{0}}}

commutes by definition. Thus
\delta_{w,T_{0}}
equals
\delta_{w,T}
, viewed as elements in
\operatorname{End}_{{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma})^{W^{\sigma}}}({\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma}))

and the result follows.
∎

We have the following facts on the formal neighborhood of a closed point in
T_{\sigma}
.
For any point
x\in T_{\sigma}
, the translation by
x
provides an isomorphism
T_{\sigma,x}^{\wedge}\simeq T_{\sigma,1}^{\wedge}
. For
w\in W^{\sigma}
, the
w
-action on
T_{\sigma}
induces an isomorphism
T_{\sigma,x}^{\wedge}\operatorname{\xrightarrow{\sim}}T_{\sigma,w(x)}^{\wedge}
and the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{\sigma,1}^{\wedge}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{\sigma,x}^{\wedge}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{\sigma,1}^{\wedge}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{\sigma,w(x)}^{\wedge}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot x}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot w(x)}

commutes.
In particular, there is a
W^{\sigma}_{x}
-action on
T_{\sigma,x}
and
T_{\sigma,1}
and the translation isomorphism is
W^{\sigma}_{x}
-equivariant.
Similarly we have
\mathfrak{t}_{\sigma.\xi}^{\wedge}\simeq\mathfrak{t}_{\sigma,0}^{\wedge}
for any
\xi\in\mathfrak{t}_{\sigma}
. Moreover, we have
T_{\sigma,1}^{\wedge}\simeq\mathfrak{t}_{\sigma,0}^{\wedge}
via the formal exponential and the isomorphism is
W^{\sigma}
-equivariant. Thus we have
{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma})_{x}^{\wedge}\simeq{\mathcal{O}}(\mathfrak{t}_{\sigma})_{\xi}^{\wedge}
naturally. We will denote these formal neighborhoods by
\hat{\mathfrak{t}}_{\sigma}
if there is no ambiguity.

The following lemma shows that the formal neighborhood of any
W^{\sigma}
-orbit in
T_{\sigma}
is isomorphic to the formal neighborhood of some
W^{\sigma}
-orbit in
\mathfrak{t}_{\sigma}
.

Lemma 2.5.4
.

Assume that
G
is simply connected.
For each closed point
x
of
T_{\sigma}
, there exists a closed point
\xi\in\mathfrak{t}_{\sigma}
such that the stabilizer subgroup satisfies
W^{\sigma}_{x}=W_{\xi}^{\sigma}\subset W^{\sigma}
and there is an isomorphism of rings

{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma})\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma}\sslash W^{\sigma})}{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma}\sslash W^{\sigma})_{\bar{x}}^{\wedge}\simeq{\mathcal{O}}(\mathfrak{t}_{\sigma})\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}(\mathfrak{t}_{\sigma}\sslash W^{\sigma})}{\mathcal{O}}(\mathfrak{t}_{\sigma}\sslash W^{\sigma})_{\bar{\xi}}^{\wedge}\simeq{\mathcal{O}}(W^{\sigma}\times^{W_{x}}\hat{\mathfrak{t}}_{\sigma})

compatible with the
W^{\sigma}
-action and the natural isomorphism

{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma})_{x}^{\wedge}\simeq{\mathcal{O}}(\mathfrak{t}_{\sigma})_{\xi}^{\wedge}
,
where
\bar{x}
denotes the image of
x
in
T_{\sigma}\sslash W^{\sigma}
and
\bar{\xi}
denotes the image of
\xi
in
\mathfrak{t}_{\sigma}\sslash W^{\sigma}
.

Moreover, in these isomorphic rings, for any root
\alpha
of
T_{\sigma}
,
1-e^{\tilde{\alpha}}(t)\in{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma})
and
\alpha\in{\mathcal{O}}(\mathfrak{t}_{\sigma})
differ only by a unit.

Proof.

We have for any closed point
x\in T_{\sigma}
,

{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma})\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma}\sslash W^{\sigma})}{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma}\sslash W^{\sigma})_{\bar{x}}^{\wedge}\simeq\prod_{x^{\prime}\in W^{\sigma}x}{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma})_{x^{\prime}}^{\wedge}\simeq{\mathcal{O}}(W^{\sigma}\times^{W^{\sigma}_{x}}T_{\sigma,x}^{\wedge})

and similarly for any point
\xi\in\mathfrak{t}_{\sigma}
,

{\mathcal{O}}(\mathfrak{t}_{\sigma})\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}(\mathfrak{t}_{\sigma}\sslash W^{\sigma})}{\mathcal{O}}(\mathfrak{t}_{\sigma}\sslash W^{\sigma})_{\bar{\xi}}^{\wedge}\simeq\prod_{\xi^{\prime}\in W^{\sigma}\xi}{\mathcal{O}}(\mathfrak{t}_{\sigma})_{\xi^{\prime}}^{\wedge}\simeq{\mathcal{O}}(W^{\sigma}\times^{W^{\sigma}_{x}}\mathfrak{t}_{\sigma,\xi}^{\wedge}).

By those discussions before this lemma,
T_{\sigma,x}^{\wedge}\simeq\mathfrak{t}_{\sigma,\xi}^{\wedge}
naturally.
Hence it suffices to find
\xi\in\mathfrak{t}_{\sigma}
satisfying
W_{x}^{\sigma}=W^{\sigma}_{\xi}
.

We claim that for any
x\in T_{\sigma}
,

W^{\sigma}_{x}
is generated by some reflections of
W^{\sigma}
.
When the root system
(T_{\sigma},R^{\sigma})
is simply connected, denote by
G_{\sigma}
the reductive group corresponding to the root system. By
[
16
]
, Theorem 8.1 applying on

G_{\sigma}
and its
x
-conjugation, the centralizer
C_{G_{\sigma}}(x)
is a connected reductive group. Its Weyl group is
N_{C_{G_{\sigma}(x)}}(T_{\sigma})/T_{\sigma}\simeq W^{\sigma}_{x}
. Thus
W^{\sigma}_{x}
is generated by reflections.
If the root system
(T_{\sigma},R^{\sigma})
is not simply connected, by classification of the pair
(G,\sigma)
(
[
17
]
, Lemma 5.1.1), a simple component of
G_{\sigma}
is of type
\mathrm{B}_{n}
. In this case, replacing the roots in the root system
(T_{\sigma},R^{\sigma})
via
\alpha\mapsto\tilde{\alpha}
, we obtain a simply connected root system
(T_{\sigma},R^{\prime\sigma})
with the same Weyl group. Thus we reduce to the simply connected case and the claim is true.

Denote by
s_{1},\cdots,s_{n}
the reflections generating
W^{\sigma}_{x}
.
We may take
\xi
such that it lies in the subspace
\bigcap_{1\leq i\leq n}\{s_{i}(x)=x\}
but it does not lie in the hyperplane of any other reflection which is linearly independent of
s_{1},\cdots,s_{n}
. Then we have
W^{\sigma}_{\xi}=W^{\sigma}_{x}
and the formula in the lemma holds.

For any
\alpha
,
1-e^{\tilde{\alpha}}(t)
and
\alpha
differ only by a unit in the ring
{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma})_{x}^{\wedge}\simeq{\mathcal{O}}(\mathfrak{t}_{\sigma})_{\xi}^{\wedge}
. Thus the second statement holds.
∎

Now we can prove that the endomorphism ring is generated by the Demazure operators.

Lemma 2.5.5
.

Viewing
\delta_{w}
’s as elements in
\operatorname{End}(\operatorname{eis}_{B}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}})
, via Lemma
2.5.3
,
the set
\{\delta_{w}\}_{w\in W^{\sigma}}
forms an
{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma})
-basis of

\operatorname{End}(\operatorname{eis}_{B}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}})\simeq\operatorname{End}_{T\sigma\sslash^{\prime}N_{G}(T\sigma)}(p_{T*}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}}),

viewed as an
O(T_{\sigma})
-module by multiplication on the target.

Proof.

As the highest term of
\delta_{w}
is a (fraction) multiple of
w
, the set
\{\delta_{w}\}_{w\in W^{\sigma}}
is linearly independent.
Then we prove that
\{\delta_{w}\}_{w\in W^{\sigma}}
is a set of generators.

We firstly prove the case that
G
is simply connected, i.e., that

{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma})\{\delta_{w}\}_{w\in W^{\sigma}}=\operatorname{End}_{{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma})^{W^{\sigma}}}({\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma}))
. It suffices to prove that for each closed point
x
of
T_{\sigma}
, denoting by
\bar{x}
the image of
x
in
T_{\sigma}\sslash W^{\sigma}
, we have an equality of completions at
\bar{x}
:

{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma})\{\delta_{w}\}_{w\in W^{\sigma}}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma}\sslash W^{\sigma})}{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma}\sslash W^{\sigma})_{\bar{x}}^{\wedge}=\operatorname{End}_{{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma})^{W^{\sigma}}}({\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma}))\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma}\sslash W^{\sigma})}{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma}\sslash W^{\sigma})_{\bar{x}}^{\wedge}.

(5)

By Lemma
2.5.4
, there exists
\xi\in\mathfrak{t}_{\sigma}
and isomorphisms

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}(\mathfrak{t}_{\sigma})\{\delta_{w}\}_{w\in W^{\sigma}}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}(\mathfrak{t}_{\sigma}\sslash W^{\sigma})}{\mathcal{O}}(\mathfrak{t}_{\sigma}\sslash W^{\sigma})_{\bar{\xi}}^{\wedge}

\displaystyle\simeq{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma})\{\delta_{w}\}_{w\in W^{\sigma}}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma}\sslash W^{\sigma})}{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma}\sslash W^{\sigma})_{\bar{x}}^{\wedge}

\displaystyle\operatorname{End}_{{\mathcal{O}}(\mathfrak{t}_{\sigma}\sslash W^{\sigma})}({\mathcal{O}}(\mathfrak{t}_{\sigma}))\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}(\mathfrak{t}_{\sigma}\sslash W^{\sigma})}{\mathcal{O}}(\mathfrak{t}_{\sigma}\sslash W^{\sigma})_{\bar{\xi}}^{\wedge}

\displaystyle\simeq\operatorname{End}_{{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma})^{W^{\sigma}}}({\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma}))\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma}\sslash W^{\sigma})}{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma}\sslash W^{\sigma})_{\bar{x}}^{\wedge}.

Thus it suffices to prove the similar equality for
\mathfrak{t}_{\sigma}
.

Again by Lemma
2.5.4
and induction on the length of
w
, we have that for all
w\in W^{\sigma}
,
\{\delta_{w}\}_{w\in W^{\sigma}}
and
\{D_{w}\}_{w\in W^{\sigma}}
generate the same submodule of

\operatorname{End}_{{\mathcal{O}}(\mathfrak{t}_{\sigma}\sslash W^{\sigma})}({\mathcal{O}}(\mathfrak{t}_{\sigma}))\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}(\mathfrak{t}_{\sigma}\sslash W^{\sigma})}{\mathcal{O}}(\mathfrak{t}_{\sigma}\sslash W^{\sigma})_{\bar{\xi}}^{\wedge}

Thus the left hand side of
5
is isomorphic to

{\mathcal{O}}(\mathfrak{t}_{\sigma})\{D_{w}\}_{w\in W^{\sigma}}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}(\mathfrak{t}_{\sigma}\sslash W^{\sigma})}{\mathcal{O}}(\mathfrak{t}_{\sigma}\sslash W^{\sigma})_{\bar{\xi}}^{\wedge}.

By Demazure’s theorem (
[
4
]
, Théorème 2),
we have

{\mathcal{O}}(\mathfrak{t}_{\sigma})\{D_{w}\}_{w\in W^{\sigma}}=\operatorname{End}_{{\mathcal{O}}(\mathfrak{t}_{\sigma}\sslash W^{\sigma})}({\mathcal{O}}(\mathfrak{t}_{\sigma})).

Thus the identity
5
holds by taking the completion at
\bar{\xi}
on the above formula.

Then we prove the case for general
G
. Take a simply connected cover
G_{0}\to G
.
Recall that we have an identity

\operatorname{End}_{T\sigma\sslash^{\prime}N_{G}(T\sigma)}(p_{T*}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}})\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma})}{\mathcal{O}}(T_{0,\sigma})\simeq\operatorname{End}_{T_{0}\sigma\sslash N_{G_{0}}(T_{0}\sigma)}(p_{T_{0}*}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{0,\sigma}})=\operatorname{End}_{{\mathcal{O}}(T_{0,\sigma})^{W^{\sigma}}}({\mathcal{O}}(T_{0,\sigma}))

and under this identity the operators
\delta_{w}
for
T
and
\delta_{w}
for
T_{0}
are equal by Lemma
2.5.3
.
To prove that
{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma})\{\delta_{w}\}_{w\in W^{\sigma}}=\operatorname{End}_{T\sigma\sslash^{\prime}N_{G}(T\sigma)}(p_{T*}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}})
, it suffices to prove that it is so after applying
(-)\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma})}{\mathcal{O}}(T_{0,\sigma})
. Then we reduce to the simply connected case and conclude.
∎

The following lemma describes
\operatorname{End}(\operatorname{Eis}_{B}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}})
as an
{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma})
-module by multiplication on the target.

Lemma 2.5.6
.

Recall that we have inclusions

\operatorname{End}(\operatorname{Eis}_{B}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}})\subset\operatorname{End}(\operatorname{eis}_{B}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}})\subset\operatorname{End}_{{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma})^{W^{\sigma}}}({\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma}))

in Lemma
2.4.12
and Lemma
2.5.1
.
For an element
w\in W^{\sigma}
, abbreviate the L-function

L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{{\mathfrak{n}}/w^{-1}({\mathfrak{n}})})^{-1}
by
L_{w}
. Moreover,
choose arbitrarily a reduced expression
s_{1}\dots s_{n}
of
w
. We will denote
the composition operator
L_{s_{1}}\delta_{s_{1}}\dots L_{s_{n}}\delta_{s_{n}}
in
\operatorname{End}(\operatorname{eis}_{B}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}})
by
(L\delta)_{w}
(it may depend on the choice of
s_{1},\dots,s_{n}
). Then
(L\delta)_{w}
lies in
\operatorname{End}(\operatorname{Eis}_{B}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}})

and a basis of
\operatorname{End}(\operatorname{Eis}_{B}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}})
is
\{(L\delta)_{w}\}_{w\in W^{\sigma}}
.

Proof.

For the statement that for any
w
and any reduced expression
s_{1}\dots s_{n}
,

(L\delta)_{w}
lies in
\operatorname{End}(\operatorname{Eis}_{B}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}})
,
by definition, it suffices to prove the case that
w
is a simple reflection
s
. In this case, denote
P_{s}
the parabolic subgroup of
G
corresponding to
s
and
M_{s}
the corresponding Levi subgroup. Then we have

\operatorname{Eis}_{B}^{G}=\operatorname{Eis}_{P_{s}}^{G}\operatorname{Eis}_{M_{s}\cap B}^{M_{s}}

and the L-function
L_{s}
for
G
is equal to
L_{s}
for
M_{s}
.
It thus suffices to prove that
L_{s}\delta_{s}
defines an endomorphism of
\operatorname{Eis}_{M_{s}\cap B}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}}
.
Hence by replacing
G
by
M_{s}
, we reduce to the rank
1
case.

As
{\mathbb{L}}_{G\sigma}^{g}\simeq(G\sigma)^{g}/G
,
L_{s}\delta_{s}
defines an endomorphism of
\operatorname{Eis}_{B}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}}
after restricting to the generic locus. Moreover,
in the rank
1
case, by Lemma
2.4.3
, the non-regular semisimple locus and the non-generic locus of
{\mathbb{L}}_{G\sigma}
are disjoint. Thus over the formal neighborhood of the non-generic locus,
\delta_{s}=\frac{1-s}{1-e^{-\tilde{\alpha_{s}}}}
differs with
1-s
only by a unit. As
\operatorname{Eis}_{s(B)}\simeq\operatorname{Eis}_{B}\circ s
, by Proposition
2.4.1
,
L_{s}s
defines an endomorphism of
\operatorname{Eis}_{B}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}}
over the whole space and
L_{s}\delta_{s}
defines an endomorphism over the formal neighborhood of the non-generic locus. Thus by Beauville–Lazslo gluing the result follows.

We then prove that
\{(L\delta)_{w}\}_{w\in W^{\sigma}}
forms a basis of
\operatorname{End}(\operatorname{Eis}_{B}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}})
as an
{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma})
-module. As
\operatorname{Eis}_{B}^{g}\simeq\operatorname{eis}_{B}^{g}
by Lemma
2.2.2
and

T\sigma\sslash^{\prime}N_{G}(T\sigma)\simeq T_{\sigma}\sslash W^{\sigma}
over the strongly regular semisimple locus, the inclusions in the statement induce isomorphisms
of
k(T_{\sigma})
-vector spaces

\operatorname{End}(\operatorname{Eis}_{B}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}})\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma})}k(T_{\sigma})\operatorname{\xrightarrow{\sim}}\operatorname{End}(\operatorname{eis}_{B}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}})\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma})}k(T_{\sigma})\operatorname{\xrightarrow{\sim}}\operatorname{End}_{{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma})^{W^{\sigma}}}({\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma}))\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma})}k(T_{\sigma})

and we may view them as the same vector space.
Furthermore, as
{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma})
is a torsion-free
{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma})^{W_{\sigma}}
-module,

\operatorname{End}_{{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma})^{W^{\sigma}}}({\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma}))
and hence
\operatorname{End}(\operatorname{Eis}_{B}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}})
and
\operatorname{End}(\operatorname{eis}_{B}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}})
inject into the vector space.
Then we may view
w
’s,
\delta_{w}
’s and
(L\delta)_{w}
’s for
w\in W^{\sigma}
as elements in this vector space. In this vector space
\{w\}_{w\in W^{\sigma}}
is a basis.

From the commuting relation
\delta_{s}f=\delta_{s}(f)+s(f)\delta_{s}
of Demazure operators,
(L\delta)_{w}
is a
{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma})
-linear combination of
\delta_{w^{\prime}}
’s for
w^{\prime}\leq w
with respect to the Bruhat ordering and the highest term of
(L\delta)_{w}
is
L_{w}\delta_{w}
.
Thus
(L\delta)_{w}
’s are linearly independent as

\{\delta_{w}\}_{w\in W^{\sigma}}
forms a basis of
\operatorname{End}(\operatorname{eis}_{B}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}})
as an
{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma})
-module by Lemma
2.5.5
. It suffices to prove that any element in
\operatorname{End}(\operatorname{Eis}_{B}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}})
can be written as an
{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma})
-linear combination of the set
\{(L\delta)_{w}\}_{w\in W^{\sigma}}
.
Any element
a
of
\operatorname{End}(\operatorname{Eis}_{B}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}})
can be expressed as
\sum_{w\in W^{\sigma}}a_{w}\delta_{w}
for
a_{w}\in{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma})
as
\{\delta_{w}\}_{w\in W^{\sigma}}

is a basis of
\operatorname{End}(\operatorname{eis}_{B}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}})
.
We use induction to the highest non-zero terms of the expression. Choose a maximal
w\in W^{\sigma}
such that
a_{w}\neq 0
. By definition of
\delta_{w}
, it is a
k(T_{\sigma})
-linear combination of
w^{\prime}
’s for
w^{\prime}\leq w
and the coefficient of
\delta_{w}
at
w
under the basis
\{w\}_{w\in W^{\sigma}}
is
L(0,\mathrm{Ad}_{{\mathfrak{n}}/w^{-1}({\mathfrak{n}})})
up to a unit in
{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma})
. Thus the coefficient of
a
at
w
under the basis
\{w\}_{w\in W^{\sigma}}
is
a_{w}L(0,\mathrm{Ad}_{{\mathfrak{n}}/w^{-1}({\mathfrak{n}})})
up to a unit in
{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma})
.

For an irreducible factor
f
of
L_{w}
and strongly regular semisimple
x\in V(f)
,
view it as a point in
T\sigma/T
and denote by
y
its image in
G\sigma/G
.
Recall that we have a projection
\pi_{G}\colon{\mathbb{L}}_{G\sigma}\to G\sigma/G
and by
Lemma
2.4.6
,

(\operatorname{Eis}_{B}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}})_{\pi_{G}^{-1}(y)}^{\wedge}\simeq\bigoplus_{w^{\prime}\in W^{\sigma}}\operatorname{Eis}_{B}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma},w^{\prime}(x)}^{\wedge}.

Then we have homomorphisms

\operatorname{End}(\operatorname{Eis}_{B}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}})\to\operatorname{End}((\operatorname{Eis}_{B}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}})_{\pi_{G}^{-1}(y)}^{\wedge})\simeq\operatorname{End}(\bigoplus_{w^{\prime}\in W^{\sigma}}\operatorname{Eis}_{B}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma},w^{\prime}(x)}^{\wedge})\to\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{Eis}_{B}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma},w^{-1}(x)}^{\wedge},\operatorname{Eis}_{B}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma},x}^{\wedge}).

In the rightmost space, only the term at
w
contributes. Thus
a_{w}L(0,\mathrm{Ad}_{{\mathfrak{n}}/w^{-1}({\mathfrak{n}})})
defines a homomorphism in

\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{Eis}_{B}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma},w^{-1}(x)}^{\wedge},\operatorname{Eis}_{B}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma},x}^{\wedge})
.
By the same local calculations in the proof of Proposition
2.4.1
,
this condition is equivalent to that
a_{w}L(0,\mathrm{Ad}_{{\mathfrak{n}}/w^{-1}({\mathfrak{n}})})
is divisible by
L_{w}
in the completion
{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma})_{x}^{\wedge}
. Varying strongly regular semisimple
x\in V(f)
, we see that
a_{w}L(0,\mathrm{Ad}_{{\mathfrak{n}}/w^{-1}({\mathfrak{n}})})
is divisible by
f
as a fraction. Then varying
f\mid L(1,\mathrm{Ad}_{{\mathfrak{n}}/w({\mathfrak{n}})})^{-1}
,
we have
a_{w}L(0,\mathrm{Ad}_{{\mathfrak{n}}/w^{-1}({\mathfrak{n}})})
is divisible by
L_{w}
and thus
a_{w}
is divisible by
L_{w}
in
{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma})
. Thus
a-a_{w}(L_{w})^{-1}(L\delta)_{w}
also lies in
\operatorname{End}(\operatorname{Eis}_{B}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}})
and has smaller highest terms with respect to the basis
\{\delta_{w}\}_{w\in W^{\sigma}}
and we apply the inductive hypothesis to conclude.
∎

Now we construct the left adjoint functor of
\operatorname{Eis}
in the next propositions. We will consider the
{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma})
-bimodule structures
of
\operatorname{End}(\operatorname{eis}_{B}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}})
and
\operatorname{End}(\operatorname{Eis}_{B}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}})
,
where the left
{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma})
-module structures are defined to be the action on the targets and the right
{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma})
-structures are the action on the sources.

We firstly consider the
\chi=1
case.

Proposition 2.5.7
.

Denote by
D\operatorname{Coh}({\mathbb{L}}_{G\sigma})_{T,1}
the thick subcategory of
D\operatorname{Coh}({\mathbb{L}}_{G\sigma})
generated by the image by
\operatorname{Eis}
(which is independent of the choice of
B
) of
D\operatorname{Coh}({\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma})_{1}
. Similarly, we define
D\operatorname{Coh}(G\sigma/G)_{T,1}
for
\operatorname{eis}
.

The left adjoint functor (in the
\infty
-categorical sense) of
\operatorname{eis}_{B}
,

\operatorname{ct}_{B}\colon D\operatorname{Coh}(G\sigma/G)_{T,1}\to D\operatorname{Coh}(T\sigma/T)_{1}

exists and more precisely we have for
{\mathcal{F}}\in D\operatorname{Coh}(T\sigma/T)_{1}
,

p_{T}^{*}p_{T*}{\mathcal{F}}\simeq\operatorname{ct}_{B}\circ\operatorname{eis}_{B}({\mathcal{F}}),

where
p_{T}
is the projection
T\sigma\sslash T\to T\sigma\sslash^{\prime}N_{G}(T\sigma)
.
Moreover, the left adjoint functor (in the
\infty
-categorical sense) of
\operatorname{Eis}_{B}
,

\operatorname{CT}_{B}\colon D\operatorname{Coh}({\mathbb{L}}_{G\sigma})_{T,1}\to D\operatorname{Coh}({\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma})_{1}

exists and for a locally free sheaf
{\mathcal{F}}
on
T_{\sigma}
,
there is a functorial inclusion of locally free sheaves (concentrated in degree
0
)
of the same rank:

p_{T}^{*}p_{T*}{\mathcal{F}}\subset\operatorname{CT}_{B}\circ\operatorname{Eis}_{B}({\mathcal{F}})

which induces an
{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma})
-bimodule homomorphism on global sections. Here the left
{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma})
-module structures of
p_{T}^{*}p_{T*}{\mathcal{F}}
and
\operatorname{CT}_{B}\circ\operatorname{Eis}_{B}({\mathcal{F}})
are defined to be induced by the
{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}}
-coherent sheaf structure and the right
{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma})
-module structures are induced by the
{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma})
-action on
{\mathcal{F}}
.

More precisely, the global section
\Gamma(p_{T}^{*}p_{T*}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}})

is isomorphic to
{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma}\times_{T\sigma\sslash^{\prime}N_{G}(T\sigma)}T_{\sigma})

as
{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma})
-bimodules.
As we have (equivariantly as
{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma})
-bimodules)

\operatorname{Hom}_{{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma})}({\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma}\times_{T\sigma\sslash^{\prime}N_{G}(T\sigma)}T_{\sigma}),{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma}))\simeq\operatorname{End}_{T\sigma\sslash^{\prime}N_{G}(T\sigma)}(p_{T*}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}})

and
\operatorname{End}(p_{T*}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}})
and
{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma}\times_{T\sigma\sslash^{\prime}N_{G}(T\sigma)}T_{\sigma})
are free
{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma})
-modules,
{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma}\times_{T\sigma\sslash^{\prime}N_{G}(T\sigma)}T_{\sigma})
can be
viewed as the
{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma})
-dual of
\operatorname{End}(p_{T*}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}})
.
Then the global section
\Gamma(\operatorname{CT}_{B}\circ\operatorname{Eis}_{B}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}})
can be identified with the
{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma})
-submodule of the
k(T_{\sigma})
-vector space
{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma}\times_{T\sigma\sslash^{\prime}N_{G}(T\sigma)}T_{\sigma})\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma})}k(T_{\sigma})
generated by the dual basis
\{(L\delta)_{w}^{*}\}_{w\in W^{\sigma}}
of
\{(L\delta)_{w}\}_{w\in W^{\sigma}}
.

Proof.

We firstly prove the argument for
\operatorname{eis}
and
\operatorname{ct}
. Choose a simply connected cover

G_{0}\to G
. Recall the morphisms
\beta\colon G\sigma/G_{0}\to G\sigma/G
and
\pi_{T}\colon G\sigma/G_{0}\to T\sigma\sslash^{\prime}N_{G}(T\sigma)
.
By the proof of Lemma
2.5.1
, the unit map
\mathbb{1}\to\pi_{T*}\pi_{T}^{*}
is an isomorphism and
\pi_{T}^{*}
is fully faithful. Thus the left adjoint of
\pi_{T}^{*}
in the subcategory of essential image of
\pi_{T}^{*}
is
\pi_{T*}
.
By Lemma
2.3.9
, we have

\beta^{*}\operatorname{eis}_{B}\mathcal{F}\simeq\pi_{T}^{*}p_{T*}\mathcal{F}

and thus
\beta^{*}D\operatorname{Coh}(G\sigma/G)_{T,1}\subset\pi_{T}^{*}D\operatorname{Coh}(T\sigma\sslash^{\prime}N_{G}(T\sigma))
. We claim that
p_{T}^{*}\pi_{T*}\beta^{*}
is the desired
\operatorname{ct}_{B}
.
Indeed, for
{\mathcal{F}}\in D\operatorname{Coh}(G\sigma/G)_{T,1}
and
{\mathcal{G}}\in D\operatorname{Coh}(T_{\sigma})
,

\operatorname{Hom}(p_{T}^{*}\pi_{T*}\beta^{*}{\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})\simeq\operatorname{Hom}(\beta^{*}{\mathcal{F}},\pi_{T}^{*}p_{T*}{\mathcal{G}})\simeq\operatorname{Hom}(\beta^{*}{\mathcal{F}},\beta^{*}\operatorname{eis}_{B}{\mathcal{G}})\simeq\operatorname{Hom}({\mathcal{F}},\operatorname{eis}_{B}{\mathcal{G}}).

Thus
\operatorname{ct}_{B}\operatorname{eis}_{B}\simeq p_{T}^{*}p_{T*}
and
\Gamma(\operatorname{ct}_{B}\operatorname{eis}_{B}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}})

is isomorphic to
{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma}\times_{T\sigma\sslash^{\prime}N_{G}(T\sigma)}T_{\sigma})
.

To construct
\operatorname{CT}_{B}
, we firstly construct

\operatorname{CT}_{B}
of
\operatorname{Eis}_{B}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}}
.
By Lemma
2.5.6
, we have that (equivariantly as
{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma})
-bimodules)

\operatorname{End}(\operatorname{Eis}_{B}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}})\subset\operatorname{End}(\operatorname{eis}_{B}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}})\simeq\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{ct}_{B}\circ\operatorname{eis}_{B}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}},{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}})\simeq\operatorname{Hom}_{{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma})}({\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma}\times_{T\sigma\sslash^{\prime}N_{G}(T\sigma)}T_{\sigma}),{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma})).

Thus we construct
\operatorname{CT}_{B}\circ\operatorname{Eis}_{B}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}}
as the coherent sheaf
corresponding to the
{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma})
-module

\operatorname{Hom}_{{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma})}(\operatorname{End}(\operatorname{Eis}_{B}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}}),{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma}))\supset\Gamma(\operatorname{ct}_{B}\circ\operatorname{eis}_{B}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}}),

which inherits an
{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\sigma})
-bimodule structure. The statement about the basis of
\operatorname{CT}_{B}\circ\operatorname{Eis}_{B}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}}
follows from
Lemma
2.5.6
. Thus we prove the statement for
{\mathcal{F}}={\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}}
.

This construction extends to the whole
D\operatorname{Coh}({\mathbb{L}}_{G\sigma})_{T,1}
by linearity: For a general element
{\mathcal{G}}_{\bullet}
in
D\operatorname{Coh}({\mathbb{L}}_{G\sigma})_{T,1}
, it can be written as a complex
{\mathcal{G}}_{\bullet}=\{{\mathcal{G}}_{n},\delta_{n}\}
where
{\mathcal{G}}_{n}=\operatorname{Eis}_{B}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}}^{k_{n}}
for all
n
. Then we may construct the complex
\operatorname{CT}_{B}({\mathcal{G}})_{\bullet}=\{\operatorname{CT}_{B}({\mathcal{G}}_{n}),\operatorname{CT}_{B}(\delta_{n})\}
. Here we construct
\operatorname{CT}_{B}(\delta_{n})
in the following formal process.
For any two categories
{\mathcal{C}}
and
{\mathcal{D}}
, a functor
G\colon{\mathcal{D}}\to{\mathcal{C}}
and
c\in{\mathcal{C}}
,
F(c)
exists if and only if
\operatorname{Hom}(c,G(-))\in\mathsf{Fun}({\mathcal{D}},\mathsf{Ani})
is representable. Thus for
c_{1},c_{2}\in{\mathcal{C}}
such that
F(c_{1})
,
F(c_{2})
exists,

c_{1}\to c_{2}
induces a morphism
\operatorname{Hom}(c_{2},G(-))\to\operatorname{Hom}(c_{1},G(-))
and then a morphism
F(c_{1})\to F(c_{2})
by Yoneda lemma.
Then we have isomorphisms

R\operatorname{Hom}((\operatorname{CT}_{B}{\mathcal{G}})_{\bullet},{\mathcal{F}})\simeq(\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{CT}_{B}{\mathcal{G}},{\mathcal{F}}))_{\bullet}\simeq(\operatorname{Hom}({\mathcal{G}},\operatorname{Eis}_{B}{\mathcal{F}}))_{\bullet}\simeq R\operatorname{Hom}({\mathcal{G}}_{\bullet},\operatorname{Eis}_{B}{\mathcal{F}}),

where the first isomorphism is from that
\operatorname{CT}_{B}{\mathcal{G}}
is locally free, the second is from the adjunction and the third is from that
R\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{Eis}_{B}{\mathcal{G}},\operatorname{Eis}_{B}{\mathcal{F}})
is concentrated in degree
0
by Lemma
2.4.12
.
Hence
(\operatorname{CT}_{B}{\mathcal{G}})_{\bullet}
is the desired object
\operatorname{CT}_{B}({\mathcal{G}}_{\bullet})

and we prove the existence of
\operatorname{CT}_{B}
.
The statement about the inclusion
\operatorname{ct}_{B}\operatorname{eis}_{B}\subset\operatorname{CT}_{B}\operatorname{Eis}_{B}

follows from the case for
{\mathcal{F}}={\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\sigma}}
.
∎

Next we construct
\operatorname{CT}
for general
\chi
.

Proposition 2.5.8
.

Let
\chi
be a character in
X^{*}(T^{\sigma})^{W^{\sigma}}
.
Denote by
D\operatorname{Coh}({\mathbb{L}}_{G\sigma})_{T,\chi}
the thick subcategory of
D\operatorname{Coh}({\mathbb{L}}_{G\sigma})
generated by the image by
\operatorname{Eis}
(which is independent of the choice of
B
) of
D\operatorname{Coh}({\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma})_{\chi}
. Similarly, we define
D\operatorname{Coh}(G\sigma/G)_{T,\chi}
for
\operatorname{eis}
.

The left adjoint functor (in the
\infty
-categorical sense) of
\operatorname{Eis}_{B}
,

\operatorname{CT}_{B}\colon D\operatorname{Coh}({\mathbb{L}}_{G\sigma})_{T,\chi}\to D\operatorname{Coh}({\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma})_{\chi}

and the left adjoint functor (in the
\infty
-categorical sense) of
\operatorname{eis}_{B}
,

\operatorname{ct}_{B}\colon D\operatorname{Coh}(G\sigma/G)_{T,\chi}\to D\operatorname{Coh}(T\sigma/T)_{\chi}

exist. More precisely, for any
{\mathcal{F}}\in D\operatorname{Coh}({\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma})_{1}
, we have

\operatorname{CT}_{B}(\operatorname{Eis}_{B}{\mathcal{F}}_{\chi})\simeq(\operatorname{CT}_{B}\operatorname{Eis}_{B}{\mathcal{F}})_{\chi}

and similarly for
\operatorname{eis}
.

Proof.

We firstly prove that
(\operatorname{CT}_{B}\operatorname{Eis}_{B}{\mathcal{F}})_{\chi}
is the desired object

\operatorname{CT}_{B}(\operatorname{Eis}_{B}{\mathcal{F}}_{\chi})
for any
{\mathcal{F}}\in D\operatorname{Coh}({\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma})_{1}
.
Indeed, for any
{\mathcal{G}}\in D\operatorname{Coh}({\mathbb{L}}_{T\sigma})_{1}
, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}((\operatorname{CT}_{B}\operatorname{Eis}_{B}{\mathcal{F}})_{\chi},{\mathcal{G}}_{\chi})\simeq\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{CT}_{B}\operatorname{Eis}_{B}{\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})\simeq\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{Eis}_{B}{\mathcal{F}},\operatorname{Eis}_{B}{\mathcal{G}})\simeq\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{Eis}_{B}{\mathcal{F}}_{\chi},\operatorname{Eis}_{B}{\mathcal{G}}_{\chi}),

where the last isomorphism is from Lemma
2.4.14
.
Then by the same formal arguments in the end of the proof of Proposition
2.5.7
,
\operatorname{CT}_{B}
can be extended to the whole category
D\operatorname{Coh}({\mathbb{L}}_{G\sigma})_{T,\chi}
. The statements for
\operatorname{eis}
and
\operatorname{ct}
follows in the same way.
∎

Remark 2.5.9
.

As mentioned in the introduction, our definition of
\operatorname{CT}
is not the same as the definition of
